Thus,

|\tilde{z}-\tilde{z}^{\prime}|\leqslant 2\beta^{2}\left\lVert{(ib+D)^{-1}-(ib+D^{\prime})^{-1}}\right\rVert_{2}\leqslant 8\beta^{2}\left\lVert{D^{-1}-(D^{\prime})^{-1}}\right\rVert_{2}.

Another application of the resolvent identity yields that

(4.1)

\displaystyle(\tilde{z}-2\beta A_{\sym}+D)^{-1}

\displaystyle-(\tilde{z}^{\prime}-2\beta A_{\sym}+D^{\prime})^{-1}

(4.2)

\displaystyle=(\tilde{z}-2\beta A_{\sym}+D)^{-1}(\tilde{z}^{\prime}-\tilde{z}+D^{\prime}-D)(\tilde{z}^{\prime}-2\beta A_{\sym}+D^{\prime})^{-1}

(4.3)

\displaystyle=(\tilde{z}-2\beta A_{\sym}+D)^{-1}(\tilde{z}^{\prime}-\tilde{z})(\tilde{z}^{\prime}-2\beta A_{\sym}+D^{\prime})^{-1}

(4.4)

\displaystyle\quad+(\tilde{z}-2\beta A_{\sym}+D)^{-1}(D^{\prime}-D)(\tilde{z}^{\prime}-2\beta A_{\sym}+D^{\prime})^{-1}

Now by
Lemma
4.8
and
Lemma
4.13
(1),

\left\lVert{(\tilde{z}-2\beta A_{\sym}+D)^{-1}}\right\rVert\leqslant\frac{1}{\tilde{b}}\leqslant\frac{3\beta^{2}}{b^{3}},

and similarly for
D^{\prime}
. Hence, (
4.3
) can be bounded by

\displaystyle\left\lVert{(\tilde{z}-2\beta A_{\sym}+D)^{-1}(\tilde{z}^{\prime}-\tilde{z})(\tilde{z}^{\prime}-2\beta A_{\sym}+D^{\prime})^{-1}}\right\rVert_{2}

\displaystyle\leqslant\frac{M_{1}}{\beta^{4}b^{6}}|\tilde{z}-\tilde{z}^{\prime}|

\displaystyle\leqslant\frac{M_{2}\beta^{6}}{b^{6}}\left\lVert{D^{-1}-(D^{\prime})^{-1}}\right\rVert_{2}

\displaystyle=M_{2}n^{6\delta}\left\lVert{D^{-1}-(D^{\prime})^{-1}}\right\rVert_{2},

where
M_{1}
and
M_{2}
are universal constants. Then to estimate (
4.4
), we write

(4.5)

(\tilde{z}-2\beta A_{\sym}+D)^{-1}(D^{\prime}-D)(\tilde{z}^{\prime}-2\beta A_{\sym}+D^{\prime})^{-1}=(\tilde{z}-2\beta A_{\sym}+D)^{-1}D(D^{-1}-(D^{\prime})^{-1})D^{\prime}(\tilde{z}^{\prime}-2\beta A_{\sym}+D^{\prime})^{-1}.

We write

\displaystyle(\tilde{z}-2\beta A_{\sym}+D)^{-1}D

\displaystyle=1-(\tilde{z}-2\beta A_{\sym}+D)^{-1}(\tilde{z}-2\beta A_{\sym})

(4.6)

\displaystyle=1-(\tilde{z}-2\beta A_{\sym}+D)^{-1}i\tilde{b}-(\tilde{z}-2\beta A_{\sym}+D)^{-1}(\tilde{a}-2\beta A_{\sym}).

As before,

\left\lVert{(\tilde{z}-2\beta A_{\sym}+D)^{-1}\tilde{b}}\right\rVert\leqslant 1.

Meanwhile,

\tilde{a}=\re 2\beta^{2}\operatorname{tr}((ib+D)^{-1})=2\beta^{2}\operatorname{tr}(D(b^{2}+D^{2})^{-1})\leqslant 2\beta^{2}\operatorname{tr}(D^{-1})\leqslant 2\beta^{2}\operatorname{tr}(D^{-2})^{1/2}=\sqrt{2}\beta.

Furthermore, recall by
Lemma
4.17
, we have that
\left\lVert{2A_{\sym}}\right\rVert\leqslant 3
with probability
1-M_{3}\exp(-M_{4}n)
, and so
\left\lVert{\tilde{a}-2\beta A_{\sym}}\right\rVert\leqslant(\sqrt{2}+3)\beta
. Thus,

\left\lVert{(\tilde{z}-2\beta A_{\sym}+D)^{-1}(\tilde{a}-2\beta A_{\sym})}\right\rVert\leqslant\frac{3\beta^{2}}{b^{3}}(\sqrt{2}+3)\beta\leqslant M_{5}n^{3\delta}.

Substituting all these estimates into (
4.6
) yields

\left\lVert{(\tilde{z}-2\beta A_{\sym}+D)^{-1}D}\right\rVert\leqslant M_{6}n^{3\delta},

and of course a similar estimate holds for
D^{\prime}(\tilde{z}^{\prime}-2\beta A_{\sym}+D^{\prime})^{-1}
. Plugging these back into (
4.5
), we get

\left\lVert{(\tilde{z}-2\beta A_{\sym}+D)^{-1}(D^{\prime}-D)(\tilde{z}^{\prime}-2\beta A_{\sym}+D^{\prime})^{-1}}\right\rVert_{2}\leqslant M_{6}^{2}n^{6\delta}\left\lVert{D^{-1}-(D^{\prime})^{-1}}\right\rVert_{2}.

Therefore, overall

\left\lVert{(\tilde{z}-2\beta A_{\sym}+D)^{-1}-(\tilde{z}^{\prime}-2\beta A_{\sym}+D^{\prime})^{-1}}\right\rVert_{2}\leqslant M_{7}n^{6\delta}\left\lVert{D^{-1}-(D^{\prime})^{-1}}\right\rVert_{2}.

Finally, since
E_{\mathcal{D}_{n}}
is contractive in the
2
-norm, the map

D\mapsto E_{\mathcal{D}_{n}}\big[(\tilde{z}-2\beta A_{\sym}+D)^{-1}\big]

is
M_{7}n^{6\delta}
-Lipschitz in
D^{-1}
on the event
\{\left\lVert{2A_{\sym}}\right\rVert\leqslant 3\}
.
Hence, since this event does not depend on
D
, the centered truncated map

D^{-1}\mapsto E_{\mathcal{D}_{n}}\big[(\tilde{z}-2\beta A_{\sym}+D)^{-1}\big]\mathbbm{1}_{\{\left\lVert{2A_{\sym}}\right\rVert\leqslant 3\}}-\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}[E_{\mathcal{D}_{n}}\big[(\tilde{z}-2\beta A_{\sym}+D)^{-1}\big]\mathbbm{1}_{\{\left\lVert{2A_{\sym}}\right\rVert\leqslant 3\}}]

is
2M_{7}n^{6\delta}
-Lipschitz. Moreover, uniformly in
D
,

\left\lVert{E_{\mathcal{D}_{n}}\big[(\tilde{z}-2\beta A_{\sym}+D)^{-1}\big]}\right\rVert_{2}\leqslant\frac{1}{\tilde{b}}\leqslant\frac{3\beta^{2}}{b^{3}}=3\beta^{2}\beta^{-3}n^{3\delta},

and therefore

(4.7)

\left\lVert{\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}E_{\mathcal{D}_{n}}\big[(\tilde{z}-2\beta A_{\sym}+D)^{-1}\big]-\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}[E_{\mathcal{D}_{n}}\big[(\tilde{z}-2\beta A_{\sym}+D)^{-1}\big]\mathbbm{1}_{\{\left\lVert{2A_{\sym}}\right\rVert\leqslant 3\}}]}\right\rVert_{2}\leqslant 3M_{3}\beta^{-1}n^{3\delta}e^{-M_{4}n}.

Next, fix a maximal subset
\Omega_{0}
of
\Omega=\{D^{-1}:2\beta^{2}\operatorname{tr}(D^{-2})=1\}
that is
\beta n^{-8\delta}
-separated with respect to
\left\lVert{\cdot}\right\rVert_{2}
. Thus, every element of
\Omega
is within a distance of
\beta/n^{8\delta}
from
\Omega_{0}
. The balls centered at the elements of
\Omega_{0}
of radius
\beta/(2n^{8\delta})
are disjoint and contained in the ball of radius
3\beta/2
centered at
0
, and so
|\Omega_{0}|\leqslant(3n^{8\delta})^{n}
.

By the union bound and
Lemma
4.18
, with probability at least

1-2(3n^{8\delta})^{n}3^{2n}e^{-n^{2-16\delta}/2},

over the Gaussian matrix, we have for all
D^{-1}\in\Omega_{0}
,

\left\lVert{E_{\mathcal{D}_{n}}[(\tilde{z}-2\beta A_{\sym}+D)^{-1}-\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}E_{\mathcal{D}_{n}}[(\tilde{z}-2\beta A_{\sym}+D)^{-1}]]}\right\rVert_{2}\leqslant\frac{4\beta}{n^{8\delta}|\im\tilde{z}|^{2}}\leqslant M_{9}\beta^{-1}n^{-2\delta}

where again we have applied
Lemma
4.13
(1) to estimate
1/|\im\tilde{z}|^{2}\leqslant 9\beta^{4}/(\beta n^{-\delta})^{6}
. By our choice of
\Omega_{0}
, every
D^{-1}\in\Omega
is within a distance of
\beta/n^{8\delta}
of some
(D^{\prime})^{-1}\in\Omega_{0}
.
Therefore, when
\left\lVert{2A_{\sym}}\right\rVert\leqslant 3
, by
(
4.7
)
,

\displaystyle\left\lVert{E_{\mathcal{D}_{n}}\big[(\tilde{z}-2\beta A_{\sym}+D)^{-1}\big]-\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}E_{\mathcal{D}_{n}}\big[(\tilde{z}-2\beta A_{\sym}+D)^{-1}\big]}\right\rVert_{2}

\displaystyle-\left\lVert{E_{\mathcal{D}_{n}}\big[(\tilde{z}^{\prime}-2\beta A_{\sym}+D^{\prime})^{-1}\big]-\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}E_{\mathcal{D}_{n}}\big[(\tilde{z}^{\prime}-2\beta A_{\sym}+D^{\prime})^{-1}\big]}\right\rVert_{2}

\displaystyle\leqslant 2M_{7}n^{6\delta}\left\lVert{D^{-1}-(D^{\prime})^{-1}}\right\rVert_{2}+3M_{3}\beta^{-1}n^{3\delta}e^{-M_{4}n}

\displaystyle\leqslant 2M_{7}\beta n^{-2\delta}+3M_{3}\beta^{-1}n^{3\delta}e^{-M_{4}n}.

The last term is exponentially small in
n
, so it may be absorbed into the constants.
Therefore,

\forall D\in\Omega,\quad\left\lVert{E_{\mathcal{D}_{n}}[(\tilde{z}-2\beta A_{\sym}+D)^{-1}-\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}E_{\mathcal{D}_{n}}[(\tilde{z}-2\beta A_{\sym}+D)^{-1}]]}\right\rVert_{2}\leqslant M_{11}\beta n^{-2\delta},

with probability at least

1-M_{3}\exp(-M_{4}n)-2(3n^{8\delta})^{n}3^{2n}e^{-n^{2-16\delta}/2}.

Note since
\delta\leqslant 1/17
,

2(3n^{8\delta})^{n}3^{2n}e^{-n^{2-16\delta}/2}=2\exp(-\tfrac{1}{2}n^{2-16\delta}+8\delta n\log n+3n\log 3)\leqslant M_{12}e^{-M_{13}n}.

so the overall probability of error is bounded by
M_{14}\exp(-M_{15}n)
. In the statement, we take
C_{1}=M_{14}
,
C_{2}=M_{15}
, and
C_{3}=M_{11}
.
∎

4.4.
Evaluation of the expected resolvent by interpolation

We have now studied the behavior of the resolvent
(\tilde{z}-\sqrt{2}\beta S+D)^{-1}
, and also estimated how far
(\tilde{z}-2\beta A_{\sym}+D)^{-1}
is from its expectation. It remains to compare
(\tilde{z}-\sqrt{2}\beta S+D)^{-1}
with the expectation of
(\tilde{z}-2\beta A_{\sym}+D)^{-1}
. This we will do using the following proposition. Here we consider an arbitrary point
z
in the upper half-plane for ease of notation, but ultimately, we will plug in
\tilde{z}=ib+2\beta^{2}g_{-D}(ib)=i\beta n^{-\delta}+2\beta^{2}g_{-D}(in^{\delta})
instead of
z
.

Proposition 4.20
.

Fix
z
with positive imaginary part, let
D
be a real diagonal matrix, and let
f
be the subordination function with
g_{\sqrt{2}\beta S-D}=g_{-D}\circ f
. Then

(4.8)

\left\lVert{\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}\left[E_{\mathcal{D}_{n}}[(z-2\beta A_{\sym}+D)^{-1}]\right]-(f(z)+D)^{-1}}\right\rVert_{2}\leqslant\frac{32\beta^{4}}{n^{3/2}|\im z|^{5}}+\frac{2\beta^{2}}{n|\im z|^{3}}.

We prove this proposition using several common techniques in random matrix theory: interpolation, integration by parts, and concentration estimates. In particular, our argument is inspired by the work of Collins, Guionnet, and Parraud
[
28
]
in the setting of the GUE. It is well-known that for a polynomial
p
, the expectation of
\operatorname{tr}_{n}[f(X^{(n)})]
, where
X^{(n)}
is a GUE matrix, is equal to the trace of
f(S)
, where
S
is standard semicircular operator, plus a correction of order
1/n^{2}
, and in fact the expectation has an asymptotic expansion in powers of
1/n^{2}
, known as the genus expansion (see
[
89
]
for an introduction and historical survey). Since it is unclear how the genus expansion would help with smooth functions beyond polynomials, Collins, Guionnet, and Parraud
[
28
]
and Parraud
[
68
]
developed another asymptotic expansion formula where the terms are expressed through non-commutative derivatives of the input function
f
, which are amenable to analytic techniques. The idea of the proof is to put the GUE matrix
X^{(n)}
and the semicircular operator in the same space and study the interpolation
(1-t)^{1/2}X^{(n)}+t^{1/2}S
inspired by the free Ornstein-Uhlenbeck process. After differentiating in
t
the expected trace of
(1-t)^{1/2}X^{(n)}+t^{1/2}S
, they use integration by parts and other tricks to get a tractable expression for the
1/n^{2}
correction. A similar interpolation method was used in
[
12
, §5]
to obtain sharp non-asymptotic bounds for the operator norms of general Gaussian random matrices.

We remark that the technique of Gaussian interpolation has formed the backbone of several rigorous results in mathematical spin-glass theory
[
41
,
3
,
77
,
78
]
, in particular in the proofs of the existence of the limit of the free energy density
[
40
]
as well as the upper bounds in (both) the replica-symmetric and replica-symmetry breaking regimes
[
42
]
. The use of a
non-commutative
version of Gaussian interpolation to bound the resolvent of the “shifted” Hessian, which ultimately provides a bound on the free energy achieved by the algorithmic process, is a notable parallel in technique.

We will follow a similar strategy with the GOE rather than GUE matrix, which is slightly more complicated because the expansion will have
O(1/n)
terms. However, since our goal is only to get a concrete bound rather than a full asymptotic expansion, we will be content to integrate by parts once and then estimate the result using the Poincaré inequality (compare
[
68
, §3.1]
). In fact, rather than directly arguing with the semicircular operator
\sqrt{2}S
, we will approximate it by a Gaussian matrix
B
of size
nk
much larger than
n
, which we may regard as fixed throughout this discussion (a larger GUE matrix of size
nk
is similarly used in
[
28
, Proposition 3.5]
and
[
68
, Lemma 3.3]
).

Thus, fix
n
and
k
, and let
B
be an
nk\times nk
Gaussian matrix where again the entries are standard normal with variance
1/(nk)
. Let
B_{\sym}=\frac{1}{2}(B+B^{\mathsf{T}})
, so that
2B_{\sym}
is
\sqrt{2}
times a GOE matrix. Then we consider
A\otimes I_{k}
and
B
as elements of
M_{nk}(\mathbb{R})
. For
t\in[0,1]
and
z\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\mathbb{R}
, let

G=G(z,t)=(zI_{nk}-2\beta[(1-t)^{1/2}A_{\sym}\otimes I_{k}+t^{1/2}B_{\sym}]+D\otimes I_{k})^{-1};

we will often abbreviate
G(z,t)
to
G
for the sake of readability in our equations. Then fix another diagonal matrix
D^{\prime}
and let

h(t)=\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}\operatorname{tr}_{nk}[G(z,t)(D^{\prime}\otimes I_{k})].

Note that

(4.9)

h(0)=\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}\operatorname{tr}_{n}[(zI_{n}-2\beta A_{\sym}+D)^{-1}D^{\prime}],

while

(4.10)

h(1)=\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}\operatorname{tr}_{nk}[(zI_{nk}-\beta(B+B^{\mathsf{T}})+D\otimes I_{k})^{-1}(D^{\prime}\otimes I_{k})],

which will involve the freely independent semicircular operator after we take
k\to\infty
at the very end of the argument.

We will estimate
h(1)-h(0)
by studying
h^{\prime}(t)
. Here we rely on
Lemma
4.8
which implies that

\frac{d}{dt}G(z,t)=\beta t^{-1/2}G(z,t)B_{\sym}G(z,t)-\beta(1-t)^{-1/2}G(z,t)(A_{\sym}\otimes I_{k})G(z,t).

Thus,

(4.11)

h^{\prime}(t)=\beta t^{-1/2}\mathbb{E}\operatorname{tr}_{nk}[GB_{\sym}G(D^{\prime}\otimes I_{k})]-\beta(1-t)^{-1/2}\mathbb{E}\operatorname{tr}_{nk}[G(z,t)(A_{\sym}\otimes I_{k})G(D^{\prime}\otimes I_{k})].

We will study these terms using Gaussian integration by parts.

Lemma 4.21
.

Consider the setup above, and abbreviate
G(z,t)
to
G
for convenience. Then

(4.12)

\beta t^{-1/2}\mathbb{E}\operatorname{tr}_{nk}[GB_{\sym}G(D^{\prime}\otimes I_{k})]=2\beta^{2}\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}\bigg[\operatorname{tr}_{nk}[G]\operatorname{tr}_{nk}[G(D^{\prime}\otimes I_{k})G]+\frac{1}{nk}\operatorname{tr}_{nk}[G^{3}(D^{\prime}\otimes I_{k})]\bigg].

Proof.

First, by cyclic symmetry of the trace,

\operatorname{tr}_{nk}[G(B+B^{\mathsf{T}})G(D^{\prime}\otimes I_{k})]=\operatorname{tr}_{nk}[BG(D^{\prime}\otimes I_{k})G]+\operatorname{tr}_{nk}[G(D^{\prime}\otimes I_{k})GB^{\mathsf{T}}]

Now since
G(z,t)
is the inverse of a symmetric matrix, it is symmetric (here we mean symmetric with respect to transposition rather than Hermitian conjugation). Also,
D^{\prime}
is symmetric. Thus, since transposition preserves the trace,

\operatorname{tr}_{nk}[G(D^{\prime}\otimes I_{k})GB^{\mathsf{T}}]=\operatorname{tr}_{nk}[BG(D^{\prime}\otimes I_{k})G].

Thus, we get

\operatorname{tr}_{nk}[GB_{\sym}G(D^{\prime}\otimes I_{k})]=\operatorname{tr}_{nk}[BG(D^{\prime}\otimes I_{k})G]=\frac{1}{kn}\sum_{i,j=1}^{kn}B_{i,j}[G(D^{\prime}\otimes I_{k})G]_{j,i}.

We take expectations on both sides and then use Gaussian integration by parts:

\frac{1}{kn}\sum_{i,j=1}^{kn}\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}\left[B_{i,j}[G(D^{\prime}\otimes I_{k})G]_{j,i}\right]=\frac{1}{k^{2}n^{2}}\sum_{i,j=1}^{kn}\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}\left[\frac{\partial}{\partial B_{i,j}}\left[G(D^{\prime}\otimes I_{k})G\right]_{j,i}\right]

Using
Lemma
4.8
, we get

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial B_{i,j}}G

\displaystyle=G\frac{\partial}{\partial B_{i,j}}[\beta t^{1/2}(B+B^{\mathsf{T}})]G

\displaystyle=\beta t^{1/2}G(E_{i,j}+E_{j,i})G

\displaystyle=\beta t^{1/2}GE_{i,j}G+\beta t^{1/2}GE_{j,i}G,

where
E_{i,j}
are the standard matrix units in
M_{nk}(\mathbb{C})
. Thus,

(4.13)

\displaystyle\beta t^{-1/2}\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}\operatorname{tr}_{nk}[GB_{\sym}G(D^{\prime}\otimes I_{k})]

\displaystyle=\frac{\beta^{2}}{k^{2}n^{2}}\sum_{i,j=1}^{nk}\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}[GE_{i,j}G(D^{\prime}\otimes I_{k})G]_{j,i}

(4.14)

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{\beta^{2}}{k^{2}n^{2}}\sum_{i,j=1}^{nk}\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}[GE_{j,i}G(D^{\prime}\otimes I_{k})G]_{j,i}

(4.15)

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{\beta^{2}}{k^{2}n^{2}}\sum_{i,j=1}^{nk}\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}[G(D^{\prime}\otimes I_{k})GE_{i,j}G]_{j,i}

(4.16)

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{\beta^{2}}{k^{2}n^{2}}\sum_{i,j=1}^{nk}\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}[G(D^{\prime}\otimes I_{k})GE_{j,i}G]_{j,i},

Taking first the terms (
4.14
) and (
4.16
) that contain
E_{j,i}
, note that

\eqref{eq: matrix resolvent computation term 2}=\frac{\beta^{2}}{k^{2}n^{2}}\sum_{i,j=1}^{nk}\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}[G_{j,j}[G(D^{\prime}\otimes I_{k})G]_{i,i}]=\beta^{2}\operatorname{tr}_{nk}[G]\operatorname{tr}_{nk}[G(D^{\prime}\otimes I_{k})G].

Likewise, (
4.16
) evaluates to
\operatorname{tr}_{nk}[G(D^{\prime}\otimes I_{k})G]\operatorname{tr}_{nk}[G]
, which also equals (
4.14
). Therefore, (
4.14
) and (
4.16
) become the term
2\beta^{2}\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}\bigg[\operatorname{tr}_{nk}[G]\operatorname{tr}_{nk}[G(D^{\prime}\otimes I_{k})G]
on the right-hand side of (
4.12
) in the statement of the lemma.

Next, consider the terms (
4.13
) and (
4.15
) that contain
E_{i,j}
. Recall that
G^{\mathsf{T}}=G
, or
G_{i,j}=G_{j,i}
. Hence,

(
4.13
)

\displaystyle=\frac{\beta^{2}}{k^{2}n^{2}}\sum_{i,j=1}^{nk}\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}[G_{j,i}[G(D^{\prime}\otimes I_{k})G]_{j,i}]=\frac{\beta^{2}}{k^{2}n^{2}}\sum_{i,j=1}^{nk}\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}[G_{i,j}[G(D^{\prime}\otimes I_{k})G]_{j,i}]

\displaystyle=\frac{\beta^{2}}{nk}\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}\operatorname{tr}_{nk}[G[G(D^{\prime}\otimes I_{k})G]]=\frac{\beta^{2}}{nk}\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}\operatorname{tr}_{nk}[G^{3}(D^{\prime}\otimes I_{k})].

Similarly, (
4.15
) also evaluates to
\frac{\beta^{2}}{nk}\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}\operatorname{tr}_{nk}[G^{3}(D^{\prime}\otimes I_{k})]
. Therefore, (
4.13
) and (
4.15
) become the term
\frac{2\beta^{2}}{nk}\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}\operatorname{tr}_{nk}[G^{3}(D^{\prime}\otimes I_{k})]
on the right-hand side of (
4.12
) in the statement of the lemma.
∎

We now carry out a parallel computation for the matrix
A_{\sym}\otimes I_{k}
rather than
B_{\sym}
. In the following, recall that we identify
M_{nk}(\mathbb{C})
with
M_{n}(\mathbb{C})\otimes M_{k}(\mathbb{C})
. Let
\operatorname{tr}_{n}\otimes\id:M_{nk}(\mathbb{C})\to M_{k}(\mathbb{C})
be the partial trace map given on simple tensors by
X\otimes Y\mapsto\operatorname{tr}_{n}(X)Y
, and let
\mathsf{T}\otimes\id:M_{nk}(\mathbb{C})\to M_{nk}(\mathbb{C})
be the partial transpose map given by
X\otimes Y\mapsto X^{\mathsf{T}}\otimes Y
.

Lemma 4.22
.

Continuing with the same setup as above and again abbreviating
G(z,t)
to
G
, we have

(4.17)

\beta(1-t)^{-1/2}\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}\operatorname{tr}_{nk}[G(A_{\sym}\otimes I_{k})G(D^{\prime}\otimes I_{k})]=2\beta^{2}\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}\operatorname{tr}_{k}[(\operatorname{tr}_{n}\otimes\id)[G](\operatorname{tr}_{n}\otimes\id)[G(D^{\prime}\otimes I_{k})G]]\\
+\frac{2\beta^{2}}{n}\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}\operatorname{tr}_{nk}[(\mathsf{T}\otimes\id)[G]G(D^{\prime}\otimes I_{k})G].

Proof.

The argument is similar to the previous lemma. First, we observe that
(A\otimes I_{k})^{\mathsf{T}}=A^{\mathsf{T}}\otimes I_{k}
and
G^{\mathsf{T}}=G
, and apply symmetry of the trace and transposes to get

\operatorname{tr}_{nk}[G(A^{\mathsf{T}}\otimes I_{k})G(D^{\prime}\otimes I_{k})]=\operatorname{tr}_{nk}[(D^{\prime}\otimes I_{k})G(A\otimes I_{k})G]=\operatorname{tr}_{nk}[(A\otimes I_{k})G(D^{\prime}\otimes I_{k})G].

Therefore,

\displaystyle\operatorname{tr}_{nk}[G(A_{\sym}\otimes I_{k})G(D^{\prime}\otimes I_{k})]

\displaystyle=\operatorname{tr}_{nk}[(A\otimes I_{k})G(D^{\prime}\otimes I_{k})G]

\displaystyle=\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}A_{i,j}\operatorname{tr}_{nk}[(E_{i,j}\otimes I_{k})G(D^{\prime}\otimes I_{k})G]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}\left[\frac{\partial}{\partial A_{i,j}}\operatorname{tr}_{nk}[(E_{i,j}\otimes I_{k})G(D^{\prime}\otimes I_{k})G]\right],

where the last line follows from Gaussian integration by parts. Note that

\frac{\partial}{\partial A_{i,j}}G=\beta(1-t)^{1/2}G((E_{i,j}+E_{j,i})\otimes I_{k})G,

and so

\frac{\partial}{\partial A_{i,j}}[(E_{i,j}\otimes I_{k})G(D^{\prime}\otimes I_{k})G]=(E_{i,j}\otimes I_{k})G((E_{i,j}+E_{j,i})\otimes I_{k})G(D^{\prime}\otimes I_{k})+(E_{i,j}\otimes I_{k})G(D^{\prime}\otimes I_{k})((E_{i,j}+E_{j,i})\otimes I_{k})G.

Thus, we get

(4.18)

\displaystyle\beta(1-t)^{-1/2}\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}\operatorname{tr}_{nk}[G(A_{\sym}\otimes I_{k})G(D^{\prime}\otimes I_{k})]

\displaystyle=\frac{\beta^{2}}{n}\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}\operatorname{tr}_{nk}[(E_{i,j}\otimes I_{k})G(E_{i,j}\otimes I_{k})G(D^{\prime}\otimes I_{k})G]

(4.19)

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{\beta^{2}}{n}\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}\operatorname{tr}_{nk}[(E_{i,j}\otimes I_{k})G(E_{j,i}\otimes I_{k})G(D^{\prime}\otimes I_{k})G]

(4.20)

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{\beta^{2}}{n}\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}\operatorname{tr}_{nk}[(E_{i,j}\otimes I_{k})G(D^{\prime}\otimes I_{k})G(E_{i,j}\otimes I_{k})G]

(4.21)

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{\beta^{2}}{n}\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}\operatorname{tr}_{nk}[(E_{i,j}\otimes I_{k})G(D^{\prime}\otimes I_{k})G(E_{j,i}\otimes I_{k})G].

Note that (
4.19
) and (
4.21
) are the same up to cyclic symmetry of the trace and switching the indices
i
and
j
. Each of these two terms evaluates to

\displaystyle\frac{\beta^{2}}{n}\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}\operatorname{tr}_{nk}[(E_{i,j}\otimes I_{k})G(E_{i,j}\otimes I_{k})G(D^{\prime}\otimes I_{k})G]

\displaystyle=\beta^{2}\operatorname{tr}_{nk}[((\operatorname{tr}_{n}\otimes\id)(G)\otimes I_{k})G(D^{\prime}\otimes I_{k})G]

\displaystyle=\beta^{2}\mathbb{E}\operatorname{tr}_{k}[(\operatorname{tr}_{n}\otimes\id)[G](\operatorname{tr}_{n}\otimes\id)[G(D^{\prime}\otimes I_{k})G].

Hence, (
4.19
) and (
4.21
) together produce the term
2\beta^{2}\mathbb{E}\operatorname{tr}_{k}[(\operatorname{tr}_{n}\otimes\id)[G](\operatorname{tr}_{n}\otimes\id)[G(D^{\prime}\otimes I_{k})G]
on the right-hand side of (
4.17
) in the lemma statement. Meanwhile, (
4.18
) and (
4.20
) are equal to each other using cyclic symmetry of the trace, and each of these terms evaluates to

\displaystyle\frac{\beta^{2}}{n}\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}\operatorname{tr}_{nk}[(E_{i,j}\otimes I_{k})G(E_{i,j}\otimes I_{k})G(D^{\prime}\otimes I_{k})G]

\displaystyle=\frac{\beta^{2}}{n}\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}\operatorname{tr}_{nk}[(E_{i,i}\otimes I_{k})(\mathsf{T}\otimes\id)[G](E_{j,j}\otimes I_{k})G(D^{\prime}\otimes I_{k})G]

\displaystyle=\frac{\beta^{2}}{n}\operatorname{tr}_{nk}[(\mathsf{T}\otimes\id)[G]G(D^{\prime}\otimes I_{k})G].

Hence, (
4.18
) and (
4.20
) together produce the term
\frac{2\beta^{2}}{n}\operatorname{tr}_{nk}[(\mathsf{T}\otimes\id)[G]G(D^{\prime}\otimes I_{k})G]
on the right-hand side of (
4.17
) in the lemma statement.
∎

To summarize, the last two lemmas together with (
4.11
) show that
h^{\prime}(t)
is the difference of (
4.12
) and (
4.17
), which we want to estimate in order to prove Proposition
4.20
. We first give an estimate for the rightmost terms in (
4.12
) and (
4.17
) respectively, which are the easier terms to deal with since they already have factors of
1/n
.

Lemma 4.23
.

With the setup above, we have

(4.22)

\left|\frac{2\beta^{2}}{nk}\mathbb{E}\operatorname{tr}_{nk}[G^{3}(D^{\prime}\otimes I_{k})]|\right|\leqslant\frac{2\beta^{2}}{nk|\im z|^{3}}\left\lVert{D^{\prime}}\right\rVert_{2}

and

(4.23)

\left|\frac{2\beta^{2}}{n}\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}\operatorname{tr}_{nk}[(\mathsf{T}\otimes\id)[G]G(D^{\prime}\otimes I_{k})G]\right|\leqslant\frac{2\beta^{2}}{n|\im z|^{3}}\left\lVert{D^{\prime}}\right\rVert_{2}.

Proof.

We first note that
\left\lVert{G(z,t)}\right\rVert\leqslant\frac{1}{|\im z|}
as a direct application of
Lemma
4.8
(1). Hence,

|\operatorname{tr}_{nk}[G^{3}(D^{\prime}\otimes I_{k})]|\leqslant\left\lVert{G^{3}}\right\rVert_{2}\left\lVert{D^{\prime}\otimes I_{k}}\right\rVert_{2}\leqslant\left\lVert{G}\right\rVert^{3}\left\lVert{D^{\prime}}\right\rVert_{2},

where we use that
\left\lVert{D^{\prime}\otimes I_{k}}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}=\operatorname{tr}_{nk}(((D^{\prime})^{*}D^{\prime}\otimes I_{k}))=\operatorname{tr}_{n}((D^{\prime})^{*}D^{\prime})=\left\lVert{D^{\prime}}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}
. This easily implies (
4.22
). For the other estimate, note that

\displaystyle|\operatorname{tr}_{nk}[(\mathsf{T}\otimes\id)[G]G(D^{\prime}\otimes I_{k})G]|

\displaystyle\leqslant\left\lVert{(\mathsf{T}\otimes\id)[G]}\right\rVert_{2}\left\lVert{G(D^{\prime}\otimes I_{k})G}\right\rVert_{2}

\displaystyle\leqslant\left\lVert{G}\right\rVert_{2}\left\lVert{G}\right\rVert\left\lVert{D^{\prime}\otimes I_{k}}\right\rVert_{2}\left\lVert{G}\right\rVert

\displaystyle\leqslant\left\lVert{G}\right\rVert^{3}\left\lVert{D^{\prime}}\right\rVert_{2}.

Here we use the fact that
(\mathsf{T}\otimes\id)
is isometric with respect to
\left\lVert{\cdot}\right\rVert_{2}
since it performs a permutation of the entries of the matrix.
3
3

3

We need to use the
2
-norm here since
(\mathsf{T}\otimes\id)
is
not
isometric with respect to operator norm.
We also use the non-commutative Hölder’s inequality.
∎

In order to estimate (
4.17
) minus (
4.12
), it remains to estimate

\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}\operatorname{tr}_{k}[(\operatorname{tr}_{n}\otimes\id)[G](\operatorname{tr}_{n}\otimes\id)[G(D^{\prime}\otimes I_{k})G]]-\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}[\operatorname{tr}_{nk}[G]\operatorname{tr}_{nk}[G(D^{\prime}\otimes I_{k})G]].

This is the more challenging part, and the key idea is to understand this as a covariance and use the Poincaré inequality.

Lemma 4.24
.

\left|\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}\operatorname{tr}_{k}\Bigl[(\operatorname{tr}_{n}\otimes\id)[G](\operatorname{tr}_{n}\otimes\id)[G(D^{\prime}\otimes I_{k})G]\Bigr]-\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}\Bigl[\operatorname{tr}_{nk}[G]\operatorname{tr}_{nk}[G(D^{\prime}\otimes I_{k})G]\Bigr]\right|\\
\leqslant\frac{16\beta^{2}\left\lVert{D^{\prime}}\right\rVert_{2}}{n^{3/2}|\im z|^{5}}+\frac{16\beta^{2}\left\lVert{D^{\prime}}\right\rVert_{2}}{n^{3/2}k^{2}|\im z|^{5}}.

Proof.

Consider the mappings
M_{nk}(\mathbb{R})\to M_{k}(\mathbb{C})
given by
f(B)=(\operatorname{tr}_{n}\otimes\id)[G^{*}]
and
g(B)=(\operatorname{tr}_{n}\otimes\id)[G(D^{\prime}\otimes I)G]
, and note that

\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}\operatorname{tr}_{k}\Bigl[(\operatorname{tr}_{n}\otimes\id)[G](\operatorname{tr}_{n}\otimes\id)[G(D^{\prime}\otimes I_{k})G]\Bigr]=\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}\langle f(B),g(B)\rangle_{\operatorname{tr}_{k}}.

To apply the Poincaré inequality, we first want to verify these are Lipschitz. Using
Lemma
4.8
(2), we see that
G
is
\frac{2\beta}{|\im z|^{2}}
-Lipschitz as a function of
B
with respect to
\left\lVert{\cdot}\right\rVert_{2}
, which implies that

(4.24)

f\text{ is }\frac{2\beta}{|\im z|^{2}}\text{-Lipschitz in }B.

For
g
, we use Lipschitzness of
G
in
B
together with the behavior of Lipschitz functions under products; more precisely, given two inputs
B
and
\tilde{B}
, let
G
and
\tilde{G}
be the corresponding values of the resolvent
G
. Then

\displaystyle\left\lVert{G(D^{\prime}\otimes I_{k})G-\tilde{G}(D^{\prime}\otimes I_{k})\tilde{G}}\right\rVert_{2}

\displaystyle\leqslant\left\lVert{(G-\tilde{G})(D^{\prime}\otimes I_{k})G}\right\rVert_{2}+\left\lVert{\tilde{G}(D^{\prime}\otimes I_{k})(G-\tilde{G})}\right\rVert_{2}

\displaystyle\leqslant\left\lVert{G-\tilde{G}}\right\rVert_{2}\left\lVert{D^{\prime}}\right\rVert\left\lVert{G}\right\rVert+\left\lVert{\tilde{G}}\right\rVert\left\lVert{D^{\prime}}\right\rVert\left\lVert{G-\tilde{G}}\right\rVert_{2}

\displaystyle\leqslant 2\frac{\beta}{|\im z|^{2}}\frac{1}{|\im z|}\left\lVert{D^{\prime}}\right\rVert.

Note here that we have the operator norm rather than the
2
-norm of
D^{\prime}
. Then since the
(\operatorname{tr}_{n}\otimes\id)
is a contraction with respect to
2
-norm, we see that

(4.25)

g\text{ is }\frac{4\beta}{|\im z|^{3}}\left\lVert{D^{\prime}}\right\rVert\text{-Lipschitz in }B.

Now let
\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}_{B}
denote the expectation with respect to
B
while holding
A
fixed. Note that
B
is invariant in distribution with respect to conjugation by
I_{n}\otimes O
for any matrix
O
in the
k\times k
orthogonal group, and of course
I_{n}\otimes O
leaves
A\otimes I_{k}
and
D^{\prime}\otimes I_{k}
literally invariant. It follows that
\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}_{B}[(\operatorname{tr}_{n}\otimes\id)[G]]
is invariant under conjugation by the orthogonal group and hence is equal to a multiple of the identity; thus,

\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}_{B}[(\operatorname{tr}_{n}\otimes\id)[G]]=\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}_{B}\operatorname{tr}_{nk}[G].

Similarly,

\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}_{B}[(\operatorname{tr}_{n}\otimes\id)[G(D^{\prime}\otimes I_{k})G]]=\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}_{B}\operatorname{tr}_{nk}[G(D^{\prime}\otimes I_{k})G].

By the Poincaré inequality (
Corollary
4.16
),

\displaystyle\Bigl|\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}_{B}\operatorname{tr}_{k}[(\operatorname{tr}_{n}\otimes\id)[G](\operatorname{tr}_{n}\otimes\id)

\displaystyle[G(D^{\prime}\otimes I_{k})G]]-\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}_{B}\operatorname{tr}_{nk}[G]\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}_{B}\operatorname{tr}_{nk}[G(D^{\prime}\otimes I_{k})G]\Bigr|

\displaystyle=|\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}_{B}\langle f(B),g(B)\rangle_{\operatorname{tr}_{k}}-\langle\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}_{B}[f(B)],\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}_{B}[g(B)]\rangle_{\operatorname{tr}_{k}}|

\displaystyle=|\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}_{B}\langle f(B)-\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}_{B}f(B),g(B)-\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}_{B}g(B)\rangle|

\displaystyle\leqslant\frac{\dim_{\mathbb{R}}M_{k}(\mathbb{C})}{(nk)^{2}}\left\lVert{f}\right\rVert_{\operatorname{Lip}}\left\lVert{g}\right\rVert_{\operatorname{Lip}}

\displaystyle\leqslant\frac{2}{n^{2}}\frac{2\beta}{|\im z|^{2}}\frac{4\beta\left\lVert{D^{\prime}}\right\rVert}{|\im z|^{3}}

(4.26)

\displaystyle=\frac{16\beta^{2}\left\lVert{D^{\prime}}\right\rVert}{n^{2}|\im z|^{5}}.

We can also apply the same reasoning to the scalar-valued functions
B\mapsto\operatorname{tr}_{nk}[G]
and
B\mapsto\operatorname{tr}_{nk}[G(D^{\prime}\otimes I_{k})G]
, and thus we get

(4.27)

|\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}_{B}[\operatorname{tr}_{nk}[G]\operatorname{tr}_{nk}[G(D^{\prime}\otimes I_{k})G]]-\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}_{B}\operatorname{tr}_{nk}[G]\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}_{B}\operatorname{tr}_{nk}[G(D^{\prime}\otimes I_{k})G]|\leqslant\frac{16\beta^{2}\left\lVert{D^{\prime}}\right\rVert}{(nk)^{2}|\im z|^{5}}.

Combining (
4.26
) and (
4.27
) with the triangle inequality shows that

\Bigl|\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}_{B}\operatorname{tr}_{k}[(\operatorname{tr}_{n}\otimes\id)[G(z,t)](\operatorname{tr}_{n}\otimes\id)[G(z,t)(D^{\prime}\otimes I_{k})G(z,t)]]-\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}_{B}[\operatorname{tr}_{nk}[G(z,t)]\operatorname{tr}_{nk}[G(z,t)(D^{\prime}\otimes I_{k})G(z,t)]]\Bigr|\\
\leqslant\frac{16\beta^{2}\left\lVert{D^{\prime}}\right\rVert}{n^{2}|\im z|^{5}}+\frac{16\beta^{2}\left\lVert{D^{\prime}}\right\rVert}{(nk)^{2}|\im z|^{5}}.

By integrating over
A
, we can obtain the same inequality with
\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}
rather than
\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}_{B}
. Finally, we recall that
\left\lVert{D^{\prime}}\right\rVert\leqslant n^{1/2}\left\lVert{D^{\prime}}\right\rVert_{2}
to complete the proof.
∎

Lemma 4.25
.

|h^{\prime}(t)|\leqslant\frac{32\beta^{4}\left\lVert{D^{\prime}}\right\rVert_{2}}{n^{3/2}|\im z|^{5}}+\frac{32\beta^{4}\left\lVert{D^{\prime}}\right\rVert_{2}}{n^{3/2}k^{2}|\im z|^{5}}+\frac{2\beta^{2}\left\lVert{D^{\prime}}\right\rVert_{2}}{n|\im z|^{3}}+\frac{2\beta^{2}\left\lVert{D^{\prime}}\right\rVert_{2}}{nk|\im z|^{3}}.

Proof.

By substituting (
4.12
) and (
4.17
) into (
4.11
), we have

(4.28)

\displaystyle h^{\prime}(t)

\displaystyle=2\beta^{2}\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}\operatorname{tr}_{nk}[G]\operatorname{tr}_{nk}[G(D^{\prime}\otimes I_{k})G]-2\beta^{2}\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}\operatorname{tr}_{k}[(\operatorname{tr}_{n}\otimes\id)[G](\operatorname{tr}_{n}\otimes\id)[G(D^{\prime}\otimes I_{k})G]]

(4.29)

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{\beta^{2}}{nk}\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}\operatorname{tr}_{nk}[G^{3}(D^{\prime}\otimes I_{k})]

(4.30)

\displaystyle\quad-\frac{2\beta^{2}}{n}\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}\operatorname{tr}_{nk}[(\mathsf{T}\otimes\id)[G]G(D^{\prime}\otimes I_{k})G].

We then estimate (
4.28
) by Lemma
4.24
, estimate (
4.29
) by (
4.22
), and estimate (
4.30
) by (
4.23
), which yields the bound asserted in this lemma.
∎

Proof of
Proposition
4.20
.

Recall the values of
h(0)
and
h(1)
from (
4.9
) and (
4.10
), note that
|h(0)-h(1)|\leqslant\sup_{t\in[0,1]}|h^{\prime}(t)|
, and apply our estimate for
h^{\prime}
from Lemma
4.25
to obtain

(4.31)

|\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}\operatorname{tr}_{n}[(zI_{n}-2\beta A_{\sym}+D)^{-1}D^{\prime}]-\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}\operatorname{tr}_{nk}[(zI_{nk}-2\beta B_{\sym}+D\otimes I_{k})^{-1}(D^{\prime}\otimes I_{k})]|\\
\leqslant\frac{32\beta^{4}\left\lVert{D^{\prime}}\right\rVert_{2}}{n^{3/2}|\im z|^{5}}+\frac{32\beta^{4}\left\lVert{D^{\prime}}\right\rVert_{2}}{n^{3/2}k^{2}|\im z|^{5}}+\frac{2\beta^{2}\left\lVert{D^{\prime}}\right\rVert_{2}}{n|\im z|^{3}}+\frac{2\beta^{2}\left\lVert{D^{\prime}}\right\rVert_{2}}{nk|\im z|^{3}}.

Note that
2B_{\sym}
is
\sqrt{2}
times a standard
nk\times nk
GOE matrix. Moreover,
D\otimes I_{k}
and
D^{\prime}\otimes I_{k}
are deterministic matrices. Thus, the asymptotic freeness theorem (
Theorem
4.2
) implies that, for every non-commutative polynomial
p
, the traces
\operatorname{tr}_{n}[p(D\otimes I_{k},D^{\prime}\otimes I_{k},2B_{\sym})]
converge almost surely as
k\to\infty
to
\tau_{n}[p(D,D^{\prime},\sqrt{2}S)]
. The same also applies to the resolvent since this can be approximated by polynomials uniformly on compact subsets of the upper half-plane. Hence,

\lim_{k\to\infty}\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}\operatorname{tr}_{nk}[(zI_{nk}-2\beta B_{\sym}+D\otimes I_{k})^{-1}(D^{\prime}\otimes I_{k})]=\tau_{n}[(z-\sqrt{2}\beta S+D)^{-1}D^{\prime}].

Thus, when we take
k\to\infty
in (
4.31
), we obtain

|\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}\operatorname{tr}_{n}[(z-2\beta A_{\sym}+D)^{-1}D^{\prime}]-\tau_{n}[(z-\sqrt{2}\beta S+D)^{-1}D^{\prime}]|\leqslant\frac{32\beta^{4}\left\lVert{D^{\prime}}\right\rVert_{2}}{n^{3/2}|\im z|^{5}}+\frac{2\beta^{2}\left\lVert{D^{\prime}}\right\rVert_{2}}{n|\im z|^{3}}.

By
Theorem
4.7
, letting
f(z)
be the subordination function, we obtain

\tau_{n}[(z-\sqrt{2}\beta S+D)^{-1}D^{\prime}]=\tau_{n}[(f(z)+D)^{-1}D^{\prime}]=\operatorname{tr}_{n}[(f(z)+D)^{-1}D^{\prime}],

so that

\left|\operatorname{tr}_{n}[(\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}[(z-2\beta A_{\sym}+D)^{-1}]-(f(z)+D)^{-1})D^{\prime}]\right|\leqslant\left(\frac{32\beta^{4}}{n^{3/2}|\im z|^{5}}+\frac{2\beta^{2}}{n|\im z|^{3}}\right)\left\lVert{D^{\prime}}\right\rVert_{2}.

Taking the supremum over
\left\lVert{D^{\prime}}\right\rVert_{2}\leqslant 1
, we obtain the
\left\lVert{\cdot}\right\rVert_{2}
-norm of
\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}\circ E_{\mathcal{D}_{n}}[(z-2\beta A_{\sym}+D)^{-1}]-(f(z)+D)^{-1}
, and so (
4.8
) follows.
∎

4.5.
Conclusion of the random matrix argument

Proof of
Theorem
4.1
.

Let
b
,
\tilde{a}
,
\tilde{b}
,
\tilde{z}
be as in the statement of the theorem, namely,
b=\beta n^{-\delta}
, and
\tilde{z}:=ib+2\beta^{2}g_{-D}(ib)
where
g_{-D}(z)=\operatorname{tr}_{n}((z+D)^{-1})
, and
\tilde{a}
and
\tilde{b}
are the real and imaginary parts of
\tilde{z}
.

Item
1
stating that
2\beta^{2}\operatorname{tr}_{n}(D^{-1})
is the maximum of the spectrum of
\sqrt{2}\beta S-D
where
S
is a semicircular operator freely independent of
D
follows from
Lemma
4.12
(2). Now recall

\tilde{a}=2\beta^{2}\re\operatorname{tr}_{n}((ib+D)^{-1}),

and so

|2\beta^{2}\operatorname{tr}_{n}(D^{-1})-\tilde{a}|\leqslant 2\beta^{2}|\re\operatorname{tr}_{n}[D^{-1}-(ib+D)^{-1}]|.

We have by
Lemma
4.9
and resolvent identities that

\displaystyle\re\operatorname{tr}_{n}[D^{-1}-(ib+D)^{-1}]

\displaystyle=\operatorname{tr}_{n}[D^{-1}-D(b^{2}+D^{2})^{-1}]

\displaystyle=\operatorname{tr}_{n}[D[D^{-2}-(b^{2}+D^{2})^{-1}]]

\displaystyle=\operatorname{tr}_{n}[DD^{-2}b^{2}(b^{2}+D^{2})^{-1}]

\displaystyle\leqslant b^{2}\operatorname{tr}_{n}[D^{-3}].

This proves the asserted estimate on
a+2\beta^{2}\operatorname{tr}_{n}[D^{-1}]-\tilde{a}
since
b^{2}=\beta^{2}n^{-2\delta}
.

For
Item
2
, note that

\operatorname{tr}_{n}[P^{2}(\tilde{a}-2\beta A_{\sym}+D)^{2}]=\operatorname{tr}_{n}[\tilde{b}(\tilde{b}^{2}+(\tilde{a}-2\beta A_{\sym}+D)^{2})^{-1}(\tilde{a}-2\beta A_{\sym}+D)^{2}]\leqslant\tilde{b},

and by
Lemma
4.13
(1), we have
\tilde{b}\leqslant 2\beta^{2}b^{3}\operatorname{tr}_{n}(D^{-4})
and then we substitute
b=\beta n^{-\delta}
.

For
Item
3
, note that

P^{2}=-\im[(\tilde{a}+i\tilde{b}-2\beta A_{\sym}+D)^{-1}],

and observe that
\im\circ E_{\mathcal{D}_{n}}=E_{\mathcal{D}_{n}}\circ\im
. Letting
z:=ib
, we estimate

(4.32)

\displaystyle\left\lVert{E_{\mathcal{D}_{n}}[(\tilde{z}-2\beta A_{\sym}+D)^{-1}]-(z+D)^{-1}}\right\rVert_{2}

\displaystyle\leqslant\left\lVert{E_{\mathcal{D}_{n}}[(\tilde{z}-2\beta A_{\sym}+D)^{-1}]-\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}E_{\mathcal{D}_{n}}[(\tilde{z}-2\beta A_{\sym}+D)^{-1}]}\right\rVert_{2}

(4.33)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+\left\lVert{\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}E_{\mathcal{D}_{n}}[(\tilde{z}-2\beta A_{\sym}+D)^{-1}]-(z+D)^{-1}}\right\rVert_{2}

By
Lemma
4.19
, with probability at least
1-M_{1}\exp(-M_{2}n)
in the Gaussian matrix
A
, we have for all choices of
D
that

(4.34)

\left\lVert{E_{\mathcal{D}_{n}}[(\tilde{z}-2\beta A_{\sym}+D)^{-1}]-\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}E_{\mathcal{D}_{n}}[(\tilde{z}-2\beta A_{\sym}+D)^{-1}]}\right\rVert_{2}\leqslant M_{3}\beta n^{-2\delta},

which takes care of (
4.32
).

To estimate (
4.33
), let
f
be the subordination function as in
Proposition
4.11
; by construction
\tilde{z}=z+2\beta^{2}g_{-D}(z)
, and so
f(\tilde{z})=z
. Hence, by
Proposition
4.20
, we have

\left\lVert{\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}[(\tilde{z}-2\beta A_{\sym}+D)^{-1}]-(z+D)^{-1}}\right\rVert_{2}\leqslant\frac{32\beta^{4}}{n^{3/2}\tilde{b}^{5}}+\frac{2\beta^{2}}{n\tilde{b}^{3}}.

Then recall by
Lemma
4.13
(1) that
\tilde{b}^{-1}\leqslant 3\beta^{2}b^{-3}=3\beta^{-1}n^{3\delta}
. Also,
E_{\mathcal{D}_{n}}
is contractive in
\left\lVert{\cdot}\right\rVert_{2}
, so that

\left\lVert{\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}E_{\mathcal{D}_{n}}[(\tilde{z}-2\beta A_{\sym}+D)^{-1}]-(z+D)^{-1}}\right\rVert_{2}\leqslant\frac{M_{4}}{\beta n^{3/2-15\delta}}+\frac{M_{5}}{\beta n^{1-9\delta}}.

Since
\delta\leqslant 1/17<3/34<1/11
, we have that
3/2-15\delta\geqslant 2\delta
and
1-9\delta\geqslant 2\delta
. Therefore,

(4.35)

\left\lVert{\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}E_{\mathcal{D}_{n}}[(\tilde{z}-2\beta A_{\sym}+D)^{-1}]-(z+D)^{-1}}\right\rVert_{2}\leqslant M_{6}\beta^{-1}n^{-2\delta}.

Estimating (
4.32
) by (
4.34
) and (
4.33
) by (
4.35
) and bounding
\beta^{-1}
by a constant, we get

\left\lVert{E_{\mathcal{D}_{n}}[(\tilde{z}-2\beta A_{\sym}+D)^{-1}]-(z+D)^{-1}}\right\rVert_{2}\leqslant M_{7}\beta n^{-2\delta}.

Then taking the negated imaginary parts of the operators, we get

\left\lVert{E_{\mathcal{D}_{n}}[\tilde{b}(\tilde{b}^{2}+(\tilde{a}-2\beta A_{\sym}+D)^{2})^{-1}]-b(b^{2}+D^{2})^{-1}}\right\rVert_{2}\leqslant M_{7}\beta n^{-2\delta},

which is the desired estimate with
C_{3}=M_{7}
.

For
Item
4
, note by
Lemma
4.13
(2),

\operatorname{tr}_{n}[b(b^{2}+D^{2})^{-1}]=-\im g_{-D}(ib)\geqslant\frac{1}{2\beta^{2}}(b-b^{3}\left\lVert{D^{-1}}\right\rVert^{2})\geqslant\frac{1}{2}\beta^{-1}n^{-\delta}(1-\beta^{2}n^{-2\delta}\left\lVert{D^{-1}}\right\rVert^{2}).

Then using
Item
3
,

\displaystyle\operatorname{tr}_{n}[P^{2}]=\operatorname{tr}[E_{\mathcal{D}_{n}}(P^{2})]

\displaystyle\geqslant\operatorname{tr}_{n}[b(b^{2}+D^{2})^{-1}]-\left\lVert{E_{\mathcal{D}_{n}}(P^{2})-b(b^{2}+D^{2})^{-1}}\right\rVert_{2}

\displaystyle\geqslant\frac{1}{2}\beta^{-1}n^{-\delta}-M_{7}\beta n^{-2\delta}-\frac{1}{2}\beta\left\lVert{D^{-1}}\right\rVert^{2}n^{-3\delta}.

For
Item
5
, observe that

\left\lVert{P^{2}}\right\rVert=\left\lVert{\im(\tilde{z}-2\beta A_{\sym}+D)^{-1}}\right\rVert\leqslant\frac{1}{\im\tilde{z}}\leqslant\frac{3\beta^{2}}{b^{3}}=3\beta^{-1}n^{3\delta}.\qed

5.
Convergence to the Primal Auffinger-Chen SDE under fRSB

In this section, we analyze the steps of our algorithm and describe how it converges in empirical distribution to the solution to the primal Auffinger-Chen SDE.
To this end, for a vector
v\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
, we define
\emp(v):=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\delta_{v_{j}}
to refer to the empirical distribution of coordinates of that vector and for
Y
a random variable in
\mathbb{R}
, we let
\operatorname{dist}(Y)\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R})
denote the probability distribution of
Y
.

In
Theorem
4.1
, with high probability over
A
, we constructed, for any diagonal matrix
D
with
2\beta^{2}\operatorname{tr}(D^{-2})=1
, a positive semi-definite matrix
P(D)
whose mass was concentrated on the highest part of the bulk of the spectrum of
2\beta A_{\sym}-D
. While the normalization of
P(D)
was convenient for the analysis of resolvents in the last section, it will be helpful now to renormalize it so that the covariance matrix we use in the algorithm has normalized trace approximately
1
. In addition, to make the Taylor expansion analysis easier in the next section, we will arrange that our step is exactly orthogonal to the current position. Thus, we introduce the following quantities: For any
\sigma\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
, let

D(t,\sigma):=\left(\frac{2\beta^{2}}{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\partial_{y,y}\tilde{\Lambda}_{\gamma}\left(t,\sigma_{j}\right)^{-2}\right)^{1/2}\operatorname{diag}\left[\partial_{y,y}\tilde{\Lambda}_{\gamma}\left(t,\sigma_{j}\right)\right].

Note that by construction
2\beta^{2}\operatorname{tr}_{n}(D(t,\sigma)^{-2})=1
.

Then, let
Q(t,\sigma)
be the positive semi-definite matrix given by

\displaystyle Q(t,\sigma)^{2}

\displaystyle:=2\beta n^{\delta}\Pi_{\sigma^{\perp}}P(D(t,\sigma))^{2}\Pi_{\sigma^{\perp}}

\displaystyle=2\beta n^{\delta}\Pi_{\sigma^{\perp}}\tilde{b}(\tilde{b}^{2}+(\tilde{a}-2\beta A_{\sym}+D(t,\sigma))^{2})^{-1}\Pi_{\sigma^{\perp}},

where we use the notation from
Theorem
4.1
. As we will see below,
\operatorname{tr}_{n}(Q(t,\sigma)^{2})
tends to
1
as
n\to\infty
, and the diagonal entries of
Q(t,\sigma)^{2}
are well-approximated by
D(t,\sigma)^{-2}
.

For our algorithm, suppose
Assumption
1.4
holds, fix a step size
\eta
and number of steps
K
such that
K\eta\leqslant q_{\beta}^{*}
. For
k=0
, …,
K
, write
t_{k}=k\eta
. Define a random vector
\sigma_{k}
in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
inductively as follows: Let
\sigma_{0}:=0
, and for
k\in\{0,\dots,K-1\}
, let

(5.1)

\sigma_{k+1}:=\sigma_{k}+\eta^{1/2}Q(t_{k},\sigma_{k})Z_{k},

where
Z_{0},Z_{1}
,
Z_{2}
, …,
Z_{K-1}
are independent standard Gaussian random vectors in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
.

As in
Lemma
2.17
, let
Y_{t}^{\gamma}
be the solution to the SDE:

dY_{t}^{\gamma}=\frac{\sqrt{2}\beta}{\partial_{y,y}\tilde{\Lambda}_{\gamma}(t,Y^{\gamma}_{t})}\,dW_{t},\;\;Y_{0}=0.

where
W_{t}
is a standard Brownian motion (see
Definition
A.1
). By
Proposition
2.11
(3),
1/\partial_{y,y}\tilde{\Lambda}_{\gamma}(t,y)
is
2
-Lipschitz in space, which implies that the solution to the SDE is well-defined. For convenience, we write

v(t,y):=\frac{1}{\partial_{y,y}\tilde{\Lambda}_{\gamma}(t,y)}\,.

For the remainder of this section, we use the following abbreviated notations:

\displaystyle D_{k}

\displaystyle:=D(t_{k},\sigma_{k})

\displaystyle P_{k}

\displaystyle:=P(D_{k})

\displaystyle Q_{k}

\displaystyle:=Q(t_{k},\sigma_{k})

\displaystyle v_{k}(y)

\displaystyle:=v(t_{k},y)

We also understand
v_{k}(\sigma_{k})
to be the component-wise application of
v_{k}
to the vector
\sigma_{k}
, i.e. the
i
th component of
v_{k}(\sigma_{k})
is
v_{k}((\sigma_{k})_{i})
.

Theorem 5.1

(Convergence of empirical distribution to primal SDE)
.

Assume fRSB. Let
\delta\in(0,1/19]
. For some universal constants
C_{1}
,
C_{2}
, …, the following statement holds. Suppose that the matrix
A
satisfies the conclusions of
Theorem
4.1

Item
3
, which happens with probability
1-C_{1}\exp(-C_{2}n)
. Suppose that
\eta\in(0,1)
and
\beta\in(1,\infty)
,
\gamma>0
, and
n\in\mathbb{N}
such that
\eta n^{1/90}\geqslant 1
and

(5.2)

C_{4}^{-1}(\exp(C_{4}\beta^{2})-1)\left[C_{5}\beta^{4}\eta+C_{6}n^{-\delta}+C_{7}e^{10\beta^{2}}\gamma^{2}+C_{8}\beta^{-2}\eta^{-1}n^{-1/18}\right]\leqslant\frac{1}{8\beta^{2}}.

Let
k^{\prime}\in\mathbb{N}
with
k^{\prime}\leqslant K\leqslant 1/\eta
, and let
\sigma_{k}
be defined as in
(
5.1
)
for
k=0
, …,
k^{\prime}
. Then with probability

1-C_{9}n\eta^{-2}\exp(-C_{10}n^{2/9-4\delta}),

in the Gaussian vectors
Z_{0}
, …,
Z_{k^{\prime}-1}
from
(
5.1
)
, we have that for
k=1
, …,
k^{\prime}
,

d_{W,2}(\emp(\sigma_{k}),\operatorname{dist}(Y_{t_{k}}^{\gamma}))^{2}\leqslant C_{4}^{-1}(\exp(C_{4}\beta^{2}t_{k})-1)\left[C_{5}\beta^{4}\eta+C_{6}n^{-\delta}+C_{7}e^{10\beta^{2}}\gamma^{2}+C_{8}\beta^{-2}\eta^{-1}n^{-1/18}\right]

and

2d_{W,2}\left(\emp(\sigma_{k}),\operatorname{dist}(Y_{t_{k}}^{\gamma})\right)+(1+e^{5\beta^{2}})\gamma\leqslant\frac{1}{2\sqrt{2}\beta}.

We argue inductively that with high probability
d_{W,2}(\emp(\sigma_{k}),\operatorname{dist}(Y^{\gamma}_{t_{k}}))
is small. The bulk of the argument is for the inductive step, and during this argument we will assume that
\sigma_{k}
has already been chosen, and hence we treat it as deterministic. Then we go from
\emp(\sigma_{k})
to
\operatorname{dist}(Y_{t_{k}}^{\gamma})
in several steps. We compare the random empirical distribution of the next step
\emp(\sigma_{k+1})
with its expectation using concentration of measure (see §
5.3
). We compare the expectation with the distribution of
Y_{t_{k+1}}^{\gamma}
using the inductive hypothesis and Lipschitz estimates on
v
(see §
5.2
), for which we also need to use the control of the diagonal entries of the matrix
Q_{k}
from
Theorem
4.1

Item
3
(see §
5.1
).

We will continue to use
C_{1}
,
C_{2}
, …to denote absolute constants in each statement and
M_{1}
,
M_{2}
, …for constants in the proofs. We continue to assume
\beta\geqslant 1
.

5.1.
Controlling the diagonal of the covariance

Here we will estimate the diagonal of the covariance matrix
Q_{k}^{2}
. From
Theorem
4.1

Item
3
, we can approximate the diagonal entries of
P(D_{k})^{2}
by
b(b^{2}+D_{k}^{2})^{-1}
where
b=\beta n^{-\delta}
. We want to show this is close to
bD_{k}^{-2}
, the diagonal matrix whose entries are given by
v_{k}(\sigma_{k})
, so that the increments of
\sigma_{k}
will resemble those in the SDE. We first estimate the error from the normalization we performed on
D_{k}
to make
2\beta^{2}\operatorname{tr}_{n}(D_{k}^{-2})=1
.

Lemma 5.2
.

We have

\displaystyle\left\lVert{D_{k}^{-1}-\operatorname{diag}(v_{k}(\sigma_{k}))}\right\rVert_{2}

\displaystyle=\left|\left(\frac{1}{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\frac{1}{\partial_{y,y}\tilde{\Lambda}_{\gamma}(t_{k},\sigma_{k,j})^{2}}\right)^{1/2}-\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}\beta}\right|

\displaystyle\leqslant 2d_{W,2}(\emp(\sigma_{k}),\operatorname{dist}(Y_{t_{k}}^{\gamma}))+(1+e^{5\beta^{2}})\gamma.

Proof.

By definition,

\sqrt{2}\beta D_{k}^{-1}=\frac{\sqrt{2}\beta\operatorname{diag}(v_{k}(\sigma_{k}))}{\left\lVert{\sqrt{2}\beta\operatorname{diag}(v_{k}(\sigma_{k}))}\right\rVert_{2}}.

In general, for a nonzero vector
h
in a Hilbert space,

\left\lVert{\frac{h}{\left\lVert{h}\right\rVert}-h}\right\rVert=\left|\frac{1}{\left\lVert{h}\right\rVert}-1\right|\left\lVert{h}\right\rVert=\left|\left\lVert{h}\right\rVert-1\right|.

Applying this to
\sqrt{2}\beta\operatorname{diag}(v_{k}(\sigma_{k,j}))
yields the first equality. For the second estimate, let
\Sigma
be a random variable on the same probability space as
Y_{t_{k}}^{\gamma}
such that the marginal of
\Sigma
is
\emp(\sigma_{k})
and
\Sigma
and
Y_{t_{k}}^{\gamma}
are optimally coupled, i.e.
\left\lVert{\Sigma-Y_{t_{k}}^{\gamma}}\right\rVert_{L^{2}}=d_{W,2}(\emp(\sigma_{k}),\operatorname{dist}(Y_{t}^{\gamma}))
. Since
v
is
2
-Lipschitz by
Proposition
2.11
, we have

\left|\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\left|v_{k}(\sigma_{k})\right|_{2}-\left\lVert{v_{k}(Y_{t_{k}}^{\gamma})}\right\rVert_{L^{2}}\right|\leqslant\left\lVert{v_{k}(\Sigma)-v_{k}(Y_{t_{k}}^{\gamma})}\right\rVert_{L^{2}}\leqslant 2d_{W,2}(\emp(\sigma_{k}),\operatorname{dist}(Y_{t_{k}}^{\gamma})).

We then recall from
Corollary
2.18
that

\left|\left\lVert{v_{k}(Y_{t_{k}}^{\gamma})}\right\rVert_{L^{2}}-\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}\beta}\right|\leqslant(1+e^{5\beta^{2}t_{k}})\gamma\leqslant(1+e^{5\beta^{2}})\gamma.

Combining these estimates yields the asserted statement.
∎

Now we are ready to control the diagonal of
Q_{k}^{2}
. At the same time, we will update the properties of
Theorem
4.1
for the new normalization of
Q
for later use in §
6
.

Proposition 5.3
.

Suppose that
A
is chosen from the high probability event in
Theorem
4.1
. Suppose that
\gamma<2
and

2d_{W,2}\left(\emp(\sigma_{k}),\operatorname{dist}(Y_{t_{k}}^{\gamma})\right)+(1+e^{5\beta^{2}})\gamma\leqslant\frac{1}{2\sqrt{2}\beta}.

Then

(1)

Approximation for diagonal:

\left\lVert{E_{\mathcal{D}_{n}}[Q_{k}^{2}]-2\beta^{2}D_{k}^{-2}}\right\rVert_{2}\leqslant C_{1}\beta^{2}n^{-\delta}
.

(2)

Approximation for diagonal in square root:

\left\lVert{(E_{\mathcal{D}_{n}}[Q_{k}^{2}])^{1/2}-\sqrt{2}\beta D_{k}^{-1}}\right\rVert_{2}\leqslant C_{3}\beta n^{-\delta/2}
.

(3)

Approximation for trace:

|\operatorname{tr}_{n}[Q_{k}^{2}]-1|\leqslant C_{1}\beta^{2}n^{-\delta}
.

(4)

Hilbert-Schmidt norm of diagonal:

\left\lVert{E_{\mathcal{D}_{n}}[Q_{k}^{2}]}\right\rVert_{2}\leqslant C_{2}\beta^{2}
.

(5)

Operator norm bound:

\left\lVert{Q_{k}^{2}}\right\rVert\leqslant C_{4}n^{4\delta}
.

(6)

Approximate eigenvector condition:

\left\lVert{\left(2\beta A_{\sym}-D_{k}-2\beta^{2}\operatorname{tr}_{n}(D_{k}^{-1})\right)Q_{k}}\right\rVert_{2}\leqslant C_{5}\beta^{2}n^{-\delta}+C_{6}(\beta+\gamma^{-1})n^{-1/4+2\delta}.

Proof.

(1) By
Theorem
4.1

Item
3
, writing
b=\beta n^{-\delta}
,

(5.3)

\left\lVert{E_{\mathcal{D}_{n}}[P_{k}^{2}]-b(b^{2}+D_{k}^{2})^{-1}}\right\rVert_{1}\leqslant\left\lVert{E_{\mathcal{D}_{n}}[P_{k}^{2}]-b(b^{2}+D_{k}^{2})^{-1}}\right\rVert_{2}\leqslant M_{1}\beta n^{-2\delta}.

We define an intermediate term
\Xi
and find a common denominator

\Xi:=D_{k}^{-2}-(b^{2}+D_{k}^{2})^{-1}=b^{2}(b^{2}+D_{k}^{2})^{-1}D_{k}^{-2}.

Hence,

\displaystyle\left\lVert{\Xi}\right\rVert_{1}

\displaystyle\leqslant b^{2}\left\lVert{(b^{2}+D_{k}^{2})^{-1}}\right\rVert\left\lVert{D_{k}^{-2}}\right\rVert_{1}

(5.4)

\displaystyle\leqslant b^{2}\left\lVert{D_{k}^{-1}}\right\rVert^{2}\frac{1}{2\beta^{2}}

Now recall that
D_{k}^{-1}=\operatorname{diag}(v_{k}(\sigma_{k}))/\left\lVert{\sqrt{2}\beta\operatorname{diag}(v_{k}(\sigma_{k}))}\right\rVert_{2}
, and
v\leqslant(1+\gamma)
by
Proposition
2.11

Item
1
so that

\left\lVert{D_{k}^{-1}}\right\rVert\leqslant\frac{1+\gamma}{\left\lVert{\sqrt{2}\beta\operatorname{diag}(v_{k}(\sigma_{k}))}\right\rVert_{2}}

By
Lemma
5.2
, if
2d_{W,2}(\emp(\sigma_{k}),\operatorname{dist}(Y_{t_{k}}^{\gamma}))+(1+e^{5\beta^{2}})\gamma\leqslant\frac{1}{2\sqrt{2}\beta}
, then we obtain

\left\lVert{\operatorname{diag}(v_{k}(\sigma_{k}))}\right\rVert_{2}\geqslant\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}\beta}-\frac{1}{2\sqrt{2}\beta}=\frac{1}{2\sqrt{2}\beta}.

Hence,
\left\lVert{D_{k}^{-1}}\right\rVert\leqslant 2(1+\gamma)\leqslant 6
, and so, substituting the last two inequalities into
(
5.4
)
,

\left\lVert{\Xi}\right\rVert_{1}\leqslant\frac{4(1+\gamma)^{2}}{2\beta^{2}}b^{2}=2(1+\gamma)^{2}n^{-2\delta}.

Therefore, by the triangle inequality with the preceding bound and
(
5.3
)
and because
\gamma<2
,

\left\lVert{E_{\mathcal{D}_{n}}[P_{k}^{2}]-bD_{k}^{-2}}\right\rVert_{1}\leqslant M_{1}\beta n^{-2\delta}+2bn^{-2\delta}\leqslant M_{2}\beta n^{-2\delta}.

Furthermore, by
Theorem
4.1

Item
5
,

\left\lVert{P_{k}^{2}-\Pi_{\sigma_{k}^{\perp}}P_{k}^{2}\Pi_{\sigma_{k}^{\perp}}}\right\rVert_{2}\leqslant 2\left\lVert{P_{k}^{2}}\right\rVert\,\left\lVert{1-\Pi_{\sigma_{k}^{\perp}}}\right\rVert_{2}\leqslant\frac{C}{n^{1/2}\tilde{b}}\leqslant M_{3}\beta^{2}b^{-3}n^{-1/2}=M_{4}\beta^{-1}n^{-1/2+3\delta}.

We can bound
n^{-1/2+3\delta}
by
n^{-2\delta}
as well since
\delta\leqslant 1/17
. Hence, by triangle inequality with the two preceding bounds and the contractivity of
E_{\mathcal{D}_{n}}
,

\left\lVert{E_{\mathcal{D}_{n}}[\Pi_{\sigma_{k}^{\perp}}P_{k}^{2}\Pi_{\sigma_{k}^{\perp}}]-bD_{k}^{-2}}\right\rVert_{2}\leqslant M_{4}\beta n^{-2\delta}.

Then multiplying by
2\beta^{2}/b=2\beta n^{\delta}
,

\left\lVert{E_{\mathcal{D}_{n}}[Q_{k}^{2}]-2\beta^{2}D_{k}^{-2}}\right\rVert_{2}\leqslant 2M_{4}\beta^{2}n^{-\delta}.

This proves the first claim.

(2) By the Powers-Størmer inequality, since
E_{\mathcal{D}_{n}}[Q_{k}^{2}]^{1/2}
and
D_{k}
are nonnegative matrices, we have

\displaystyle\left\lVert{E_{\mathcal{D}_{n}}[Q_{k}^{2}]^{1/2}-\sqrt{2}\beta D_{k}^{-1}}\right\rVert_{2}

\displaystyle\leqslant\left\lVert{E_{\mathcal{D}_{n}}[Q_{k}^{2}]-2\beta^{2}D_{k}^{-2}}\right\rVert_{1}^{1/2}

\displaystyle\leqslant\left\lVert{E_{\mathcal{D}_{n}}[Q_{k}^{2}]-2\beta^{2}D_{k}^{-2}}\right\rVert_{2}^{1/2}

\displaystyle\leqslant(2M_{4})^{1/2}\beta n^{-\delta/2}.

(3) The third claim is immediate from the first claim since
2\beta^{2}\operatorname{tr}_{n}(D_{k}^{-2})=1
by construction.

(4) Note that

\left\lVert{E_{\mathcal{D}_{n}}[Q_{k}^{2}]}\right\rVert_{2}\leqslant 72\beta^{2}\left\lVert{D_{k}^{-2}}\right\rVert_{2}+2M_{4}\beta^{2}n^{-\delta}\leqslant 8\beta^{2}+2M_{4}\beta^{2}

since we have
\left\lVert{D_{k}^{-1}}\right\rVert\leqslant 6
.

(5) Note that by
Theorem
4.1

Item
5
,

\left\lVert{Q_{k}^{2}}\right\rVert\leqslant 2\beta n^{\delta}\left\lVert{P_{k}^{2}}\right\rVert\leqslant Cn^{4\delta}.

(6) Using
Theorem
4.1

Item
2
, letting
\overline{Q}_{k}^{2}:=2\beta n^{\delta}P_{k}^{2}
, we get

\left\lVert{\left(2\beta A_{\sym}-D_{k}-\tilde{a}\right)\overline{Q}_{k}}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}=\operatorname{tr}_{n}\left(\overline{Q}_{k}^{2}(\tilde{a}-2\beta A_{\sym}+D_{k})^{2}\right)\leqslant 2\beta^{4}n^{-2\delta}\left\lVert{D_{k}^{-1}}\right\rVert^{2}\leqslant 72\beta^{4}n^{-2\delta}

since
\left\lVert{D_{k}^{-1}}\right\rVert\leqslant 6
.
By the Powers-Størmer inequality,

\left\lVert{Q_{k}-\overline{Q}_{k}}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}\leqslant\left\lVert{Q_{k}^{2}-\overline{Q}_{k}^{2}}\right\rVert_{1}\leqslant 2\beta n^{\delta}\left(2\left\lVert{(1-\Pi_{\sigma_{k}^{\perp}})P_{k}^{2}}\right\rVert_{1}+\left\lVert{(1-\Pi_{\sigma_{k}^{\perp}})P_{k}^{2}(1-\Pi_{\sigma_{k}^{\perp}})}\right\rVert_{1}\right),

which is bounded above by
6\beta n^{\delta-1/2}\left\lVert{P_{k}^{2}}\right\rVert
, since
\left\lVert{1-\Pi_{\sigma_{k}^{\perp}}}\right\rVert_{2}=n^{-1/2}
.
So by triangle inequality,

(5.5)

\left\lVert{\left(2\beta A_{\sym}-D_{k}-\tilde{a}\right)Q_{k}}\right\rVert_{2}\leqslant 6\sqrt{2}\beta^{2}n^{-\delta}+\sqrt{6\beta}n^{\delta/2-1/4}\left\lVert{P_{k}}\right\rVert\left\lVert{2\beta A_{\sym}-D_{k}-\tilde{a}}\right\rVert.

Then recall from
Theorem
4.1

Item
1
that

|\tilde{a}-2\beta^{2}\operatorname{tr}_{n}(D_{k}^{-1})|\leqslant 2\beta^{4}n^{-2\delta}\operatorname{tr}_{n}(D_{k}^{-3})\leqslant n^{-2\delta}\left\lVert{D_{k}^{-1}}\right\rVert\cdot 2\beta^{4}\operatorname{tr}_{n}(D_{k}^{-2})\leqslant 12\beta^{2}n^{-2\delta}.

Hence, by
Item
3
,

\left\lVert{\left(\tilde{a}-2\beta^{2}\operatorname{tr}_{n}(D_{k}^{-1})\right)Q_{k}}\right\rVert_{2}\leqslant M_{5}\beta\cdot 2\beta^{2}n^{-2\delta}.

Therefore, applying triangle inequality with
(
5.5
)
and the preceding bound,

\left\lVert{\left(2\beta A_{\sym}-D_{k}-2\beta^{2}\operatorname{tr}_{n}(D_{k}^{-1})\right)Q_{k}}\right\rVert_{2}\leqslant 6\sqrt{2}\beta^{2}n^{-\delta}+M_{6}n^{-1/4+2\delta}(\beta+\gamma^{-1})+M_{5}\beta^{3}\cdot 2n^{-2\delta},

where we used
Proposition
2.11
(1) and the high-probability event from
Theorem
4.1

Item
3
to obtain

\left\lVert{\tilde{a}-2\beta A_{\sym}+D_{k}}\right\rVert\leqslant M_{7}(\beta+\gamma^{-1})
and
Theorem
4.1

Item
5
gives
\left\lVert{P_{k}}\right\rVert\leqslant M_{8}\beta^{-1/2}n^{3\delta/2}
.
∎

5.2.
The expected empirical distribution of the next iterate

Our next goal is, having fixed
\sigma_{k}
, to estimate the distance between the
expected
empirical distribution
\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}[\emp(\sigma_{k+1})\mid A,\sigma_{k}]
and
\operatorname{dist}(Y_{t_{k+1}}^{\gamma}\mid Y_{t_{k}}^{\gamma})
. First, to make solution of the SDE easier to compare with the result of our algorithm, we want to approximate
Y_{t_{k+1}}^{\gamma}
by
Y_{t_{k}}^{\gamma}+\sqrt{2}\beta v_{k}(Y_{t_{k}}^{\gamma})(W_{t_{k+1}}-W_{t_{k}})
. For this purpose, we use the following standard estimate for solutions to SDEs.

Lemma 5.4
.

For all
t\in[0,1-\eta]
, we have

\left\lVert{Y_{t+\eta}^{\gamma}-\left[Y_{t}^{\gamma}+\sqrt{2}\beta v(t,Y_{t}^{\gamma})(W_{t+\eta}-W_{t})\right]}\right\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}\leqslant C\beta^{6}\eta^{2}.

Proof.

Recall

Y_{t+\eta}^{\gamma}-\left(Y_{t}^{\gamma}+\sqrt{2}\beta v(t,Y_{t}^{\gamma})(W_{t+\eta}-W_{t})\right)=\int_{0\leqslant h\leqslant\eta}\sqrt{2}\beta\left[v(t+h,Y_{t+h}^{\gamma})-v(t,Y_{t}^{\gamma})\right]dW_{t+h}.

By the Itô isometry, the Lipschitz bounds for
v
(
Proposition
2.11
), and the arithmetic-geometric mean inequality,

\displaystyle\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}\left|Y_{t+\eta}^{\gamma}-Y_{t}^{\gamma}-\sqrt{2}\beta v(t,Y_{t}^{\gamma})(W_{t+\eta}-W_{t})\right|^{2}

\displaystyle=2\beta^{2}\int_{0\leqslant h\leqslant\eta}\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}\left|v(t+h,Y_{t+h}^{\gamma})-v(t,Y_{t}^{\gamma})\right|^{2}\,dh

\displaystyle\leqslant 2\beta^{2}\int_{0}^{\eta}\left(M_{1}\beta^{2}h+2|Y_{t+h}^{\gamma}-Y_{t}^{\gamma}|\right)^{2}\,dh

\displaystyle\leqslant\int_{0}^{\eta}\left(4M_{1}^{2}\beta^{6}h^{2}+8\beta^{2}|Y^{\gamma}_{t+h}-Y^{\gamma}_{t}|^{2}\right)\,dh.

Now note that since
v
is bounded by
1+\gamma
, we also have

\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}\left|Y_{t+h}^{\gamma}-Y_{t}^{\gamma}\right|^{2}=2\beta^{2}\int_{0}^{h}\left|v(t+h^{\prime},Y_{t+h^{\prime}}^{\gamma})\right|^{2}\,dh^{\prime}\leqslant 2\beta^{2}h(1+\gamma).

Therefore, we get

\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}\left|Y_{t+\eta}^{\gamma}-Y_{t}^{\gamma}-\sqrt{2}\beta v(t,Y^{\gamma}_{t})(W_{t+\eta}-W_{t})\right|^{2}\leqslant\int_{0}^{\eta}(4M_{1}^{2}\beta^{6}h^{2}+16\beta^{4}h(1+\gamma))\,dh=M_{2}\beta^{6}\eta^{3}+M_{3}\beta^{4}\eta^{2}\leqslant M_{4}\beta^{6}\eta^{2}.

∎

Lemma 5.5
.

Assume
A
satisfies the conclusions of
Theorem
4.1
. Let
\sigma_{k}
and
\operatorname{dist}(Y_{t_{k}}^{\gamma})
be fixed with

2d_{W,2}\left(\emp(\sigma_{k}),\operatorname{dist}(Y_{t_{k}}^{\gamma})\right)+(1+e^{5\beta^{2}t_{k}})\gamma\leqslant\frac{1}{2\sqrt{2}\beta}.

Then,

d_{W,2}\left(\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}\left[\emp(\sigma_{k+1})|A,\sigma_{1},\dots,\sigma_{k}\right],\operatorname{dist}(Y_{t_{k+1}}^{\gamma}\mid Y_{t_{k}}^{\gamma})\right)^{2}-d_{W,2}\left(\emp(\sigma_{k}),\operatorname{dist}(Y_{t_{k}}^{\gamma})\right)^{2}\\
\leqslant\eta\beta^{2}\cdot\left[C_{1}\beta^{4}\eta+C_{2}n^{-\delta}+C_{3}d_{W,2}\left(\emp(\sigma_{k}),\operatorname{dist}(Y_{t_{k}}^{\gamma})\right)^{2}+C_{4}e^{10\beta^{2}}\gamma^{2}\right].

Proof.

Consider the discrete probability space
[n]
with normalized counting measure, and let
\Sigma
be a random variable that takes value
\sigma_{k,j}
on point
j
, so that
\Sigma
has distribution
\emp(\sigma_{k})
. Also, let
\Xi
be the random variable that takes value
((Q_{k}^{2})_{j,j})^{1/2}
at point
j
. Let
W
be a normal random variable of variance
\eta
independent of
(\Sigma,\Xi)
. We first observe that
\Sigma+\Xi W
has the distribution
\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}\emp(\sigma_{k+1})
. Indeed, for smooth functions
f
,

\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}\frac{1}{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}f(\sigma_{k+1,j})=\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}\frac{1}{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}f\left(\sigma_{k,j}+\eta^{1/2}(Q_{k}Z_{k})_{j}\right).

Since
\eta^{1/2}(Q_{k}Z_{k})_{j}
is a normal random variable of variance
\eta(Q_{k}^{2})_{j,j}
, the expectation is the same if we replace
\eta^{1/2}(Q_{k}Z_{k})_{j}
with
((Q_{k}^{2})_{j,j})^{1/2}W
. Thus,

\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}\frac{1}{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}f\left(\sigma_{k,j}+\eta^{1/2}(Q_{k}Z_{k})_{j}\right)=\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}\frac{1}{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}f\left(\sigma_{k,j}+((Q_{k}^{2})_{j,j})^{1/2}W\right)=\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}f(\Sigma+\Xi W).

By choosing an appropriate larger probability space, we can arrange that
\Sigma
,
\Xi
, and
(W_{t})_{0\leqslant t\leqslant t_{k}}
be in the same probability space (hence also
Y_{t}^{\gamma}
is on the same probability space since
Y_{t}^{\gamma}
is obtained by solving the SDE with Brownian motion
W_{t}
) such that
\Sigma
and
Y_{t_{k}}^{\gamma}
are optimally coupled, that is,

\left\lVert{\Sigma-Y_{t_{k}}^{\gamma}}\right\rVert_{L^{2}}=d_{W,2}\left(\emp(\sigma_{k}),\operatorname{dist}(Y^{\gamma}_{t_{k}})\right).

Then take the rest of the Brownian motion
(W_{t}-W_{t_{k}})_{t\geqslant t_{k}}
to be independent of
(\Sigma,\Xi,(W_{t})_{0\leqslant t\leqslant t_{k+1}}
). Since
W_{t_{k+1}}-W_{t_{k}}
has variance
\eta
, we can identify it with the
W
from the previous paragraph.

Making the conditioning in
\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}\left[\emp(\sigma_{k+1})|A,\sigma_{1},\dots,\sigma_{k}\right]
notationally implicit for brevity, we want to estimate

d_{W,2}\left(\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}\emp(\sigma_{k+1}),\operatorname{dist}(Y_{t_{k+1}}^{\gamma}\mid Y_{t_{k}}^{\gamma})\right)\leqslant\left\lVert{(\Sigma+\Xi W)-Y_{t_{k+1}}^{\gamma}}\right\rVert_{L^{2}}=\left\lVert{(\Sigma-Y_{t_{k}}^{\gamma})+(\Xi W-Y_{t_{k+1}}^{\gamma}+Y_{t_{k}}^{\gamma})}\right\rVert_{L^{2}}.

Observe that the conditional expectation of
\Xi W
given
(Y_{t_{k}}^{\gamma},\Sigma)
is zero because
W
is independent of these variables. Moreover, since
Y_{t_{k+1}}^{\gamma}-Y_{t_{k}}^{\gamma}=\int_{t_{k}}^{t_{k+1}}\sqrt{2}\beta v(t,Y_{t}^{\gamma})\,dW_{t}
, this also has conditional expectation zero given
(Y_{t_{k}}^{\gamma},\Sigma)
. Thus,
\Xi W-Y_{t_{k+1}}^{\gamma}+Y_{t_{k}}^{\gamma}
and
\Sigma-Y_{t_{k}}^{\gamma}
are orthogonal in
L^{2}
and so

\left\lVert{\Sigma+\Xi W-Y^{\gamma}_{t_{k+1}}}\right\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}=\left\lVert{\Sigma-Y_{t_{k}}^{\gamma}}\right\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}+\left\lVert{\Xi W-Y_{t_{k+1}}^{\gamma}+Y_{t_{k}}^{\gamma}}\right\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}.

Thus,

(5.6)

d_{W,2}\left(\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}\emp(\sigma_{k+1}),\operatorname{dist}(Y^{\gamma}_{t_{k+1}}\mid Y_{t_{k}}^{\gamma})\right)^{2}\leqslant d_{W,2}\left(\emp(\sigma_{k}),\operatorname{dist}(Y_{t_{k}}^{\gamma})\right)^{2}+\left\lVert{\Xi W-Y_{t_{k+1}}^{\gamma}+Y_{t_{k}}^{\gamma}}\right\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}.

Next, we estimate by triangle inequality

(5.7)

\left\lVert{\Xi W-Y_{t_{k+1}}^{\gamma}+Y_{t_{k}}^{\gamma}}\right\rVert_{L^{2}}\leqslant\left\lVert{\Xi W-\sqrt{2}\beta v_{k}(Y_{t_{k}}^{\gamma})W}\right\rVert_{L^{2}}+\left\lVert{Y_{t_{k+1}}^{\gamma}-Y_{t_{k}}^{\gamma}-\sqrt{2}\beta v_{k}(Y_{t_{k}}^{\gamma})W}\right\rVert_{L^{2}}.

By
Lemma
5.4
, the second term of
(
5.7
)
satisfies

(5.8)

\left\lVert{Y_{t_{k+1}}^{\gamma}-Y_{t_{k}}^{\gamma}-\sqrt{2}\beta v_{k}(Y_{t_{k}}^{\gamma})W}\right\rVert_{L^{2}}\leqslant M_{1}\beta^{3}\eta.

Meanwhile, by another triangle inequality, the first term satisfies

\displaystyle\left\lVert{\Xi W-\sqrt{2}\beta v_{k}(Y_{t_{k}}^{\gamma})W}\right\rVert_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle=\eta^{1/2}\left\lVert{\Xi-\sqrt{2}\beta v_{k}(Y_{t_{k}}^{\gamma})}\right\rVert_{L^{2}}

(5.9)

\displaystyle\leqslant\eta^{1/2}\left\lVert{\Xi-\sqrt{2}\beta v_{k}(\Sigma)}\right\rVert_{L^{2}}+\eta^{1/2}\left\lVert{\sqrt{2}\beta v_{k}(\Sigma)-\sqrt{2}\beta v_{k}(Y_{t_{k}}^{\gamma})}\right\rVert_{L^{2}}.

Furthermore,
Lemma
5.2
and
Proposition
5.3

Item
2
, along with the fact that
1+e^{5\beta^{2}}\leqslant 2e^{5\beta^{2}}
, show that the first term of
(
5.7
)
satisfies

\displaystyle\left\lVert{\Xi-\sqrt{2}\beta v_{k}(\Sigma)}\right\rVert_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle\leqslant\left\lVert{E_{\mathcal{D}_{n}}[Q_{k}^{2}]^{1/2}-\sqrt{2}\beta D_{k}^{-1}}\right\rVert_{2}+\sqrt{2}\beta\left\lVert{D_{k}^{-1}-\operatorname{diag}(v_{k}(\sigma_{k}))}\right\rVert_{2}

\displaystyle\leqslant M_{2}\beta n^{-\delta/2}+2\sqrt{2}\beta d_{W,2}\left(\emp(\sigma_{k}),\operatorname{dist}(Y_{t_{k}}^{\gamma})\right)+2\sqrt{2}\beta e^{5\beta^{2}}\gamma.

Moreover, since
v
is
2
-Lipschitz in space by
Proposition
2.11
, we get for the second term

\left\lVert{\sqrt{2}\beta v_{k}(\Sigma)-\sqrt{2}\beta v_{k}(Y^{\gamma}_{t_{k}})}\right\rVert_{L^{2}}\leqslant M_{3}\beta d_{W,2}\left(\emp(\sigma_{k}),\operatorname{dist}(Y^{\gamma}_{t_{k}})\right).

Thus, combining the last two inequalities with
(
5.9
)
,

\left\lVert{\Xi W-\sqrt{2}\beta v_{k}(Y^{\gamma}_{t_{k}})W}\right\rVert_{L^{2}}\leqslant\eta^{1/2}\left[M_{2}\beta n^{-\delta/2}+M_{4}\beta d_{W,2}\left(\emp(\sigma_{k}),\operatorname{dist}(Y^{\gamma}_{t_{k}})\right)+M_{5}\beta e^{5\beta^{2}}\gamma\right],

which then combines with
(
5.7
)
and
(
5.8
)
to yield

\left\lVert{\Xi W-Y^{\gamma}_{t_{k+1}}+Y^{\gamma}_{t_{k}}}\right\rVert_{L^{2}}\leqslant M_{1}\beta^{3}\eta+\eta^{1/2}\beta\left[M_{2}n^{-\delta/2}+M_{4}d_{W,2}\left(\emp(\sigma_{k}),\operatorname{dist}(Y^{\gamma}_{t_{k}})\right)+M_{5}e^{5\beta^{2}}\gamma\right].

Finally, using the arithmetic-geometric mean inequality,

\left\lVert{\Xi W-Y^{\gamma}_{t_{k+1}}+Y^{\gamma}_{t_{k}}}\right\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}\leqslant\eta\beta^{2}\cdot\left[M_{6}\beta^{4}\eta+M_{7}n^{-\delta}+M_{8}d_{W,2}\left(\emp(\sigma_{k}),\operatorname{dist}(Y^{\gamma}_{t_{k}})\right)^{2}+M_{9}e^{10\beta^{2}}\gamma^{2}\right].

This with
(
5.6
)
shows the desired conclusion with
C_{1}=M_{6}
,
C_{2}=M_{7}
,
C_{3}=M_{8}
,
C_{4}=M_{9}
.
∎

5.3.
Concentration for the empirical distribution

Finally, we will estimate the difference between the empirical distribution of
\sigma_{k+1}
and its expectation using concentration of measure. The estimate that we obtain below is derived from concentration of measure alone, and is certainly not optimal. Obtaining optimal estimates for the Wasserstein distances for empirical distributions is a challenging problem, and readers well-versed in probability theory may try to obtain better estimates using more refined tools. Here it will be convenient to estimate the
L^{1}
-Wasserstein distance first, rather than directly attacking the
L^{2}
-Wasserstein distance. Recall that the
L^{1}
-Wasserstein distance is

d_{W,1}(\mu,\nu)=\inf\big\{\left\lVert{X-Y}\right\rVert_{L^{1}}:X\sim\mu,Y\sim\nu\big\}=\sup_{f\in\mathsf{Lip}_{1}(\mathbb{R}),f(0)=0}\left|\int f\,d(\mu-\nu)\right|,

where the last equality is by Monge-Kantorovich-Rubinstein duality; see
Corollary
B.3
.

We also use the following construction of a dense family of
1
-Lipschitz functions which is well known and easy to verify.

Lemma 5.6
.

Fix
\ell,m\in\mathbb{N}
. Let
\mathsf{Lip}_{1}([-2^{\ell},2^{\ell}])
be the set of
1
-Lipschitz functions on
[-2^{\ell},2^{\ell}]
that vanish at
0
. Then there exist
2^{2^{\ell+m+1}}
functions in
\mathsf{Lip}_{1}([-2^{\ell},2^{\ell}])
that are
1/2^{m}
-dense with respect to the uniform norm. Specifically take the Lipschitz functions that vanish at zero and are linear with slopes
\pm 1
on each interval of the form
[(j-1)/2^{m},j/2^{m}]
, for
j=-2^{\ell+m}+1,\dots,2^{\ell+m}
. The number of such functions is
2^{2^{\ell+m+1}}
since they are uniquely described by the choice of slope
\pm 1
on each of the
2\cdot 2^{\ell+m}
intervals.

This allows us to estimate the
L^{1}
Wasserstein distance using concentration if the measures are supported in a bounded interval. For this purpose, we estimate probabilities of the maximum coordinate of
\sigma_{k}
being large.

Lemma 5.7
.

Let
\alpha>0
. We have

\max_{j}|(Q_{k}Z_{k})_{j}|\leqslant n^{\alpha}

with probability at least

1-C_{1}n\exp(-C_{2}n^{2\alpha-4\delta}).

Proof.

Recall from
Proposition
5.3

Item
5
that
\left\lVert{Q_{k}^{2}}\right\rVert\leqslant M_{1}n^{4\delta}
. Therefore,
(Q_{k}Z_{k})_{j}
is a normal random variable with mean zero and variance bounded by
M_{1}n^{4\delta}
. Thus,

\mathop{{}\mathbb{P}}\left(\left|\left(Q_{k}Z_{k}\right)_{j}\right|\geqslant n^{\alpha}\right)\leqslant M_{2}\exp(-M_{3}n^{2\alpha-4\delta}).

The asserted estimate therefore follows from a union bound over the coordinates.
∎

Lemma 5.8
.

Let
Z
be a real Gaussian random variable with mean
a
and variance
b
. Then

\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbbm{1}_{|Z-a|\geqslant c}|Z-a|^{2}\right]\leqslant\frac{2b}{\sqrt{2\pi}}(b^{-1/2}c+b^{1/2}c^{-1})e^{-b^{-1}c^{2}/2}.

Proof.

Without loss of generality, assume that
a=0
. Let
\tilde{Z}=b^{-1/2}Z
which is a standard normal. Then

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbbm{1}_{|Z|\geqslant c}|Z|^{2}\right]

\displaystyle=b\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbbm{1}_{|\tilde{Z}|\geqslant b^{-1/2}c}|\tilde{Z}|^{2}\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{2b}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\int_{b^{-1/2}c}^{\infty}z^{2}e^{-z^{2}/2}\,dz

\displaystyle=\frac{2b}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\left(\left[-ze^{-z^{2}/2}\right]_{b^{-1/2}c}^{\infty}+\int_{b^{-1/2}c}^{\infty}e^{-z^{2}/2}\,dz\right)

\displaystyle\leqslant\frac{2b}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\left[b^{-1/2}ce^{-b^{-1}c^{2}/2}+b^{1/2}c^{-1}\int_{b^{-1/2}c}^{\infty}ze^{-z^{2}/2}\,dz\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{2b}{\sqrt{2\pi}}(b^{-1/2}c+b^{1/2}c^{-1})e^{-b^{-1}c^{2}/2}.\qed

Lemma 5.9
.

Let
\delta\in(0,1/19]
and let
\alpha\in(2\delta,1/7-4\delta/7)
.
Assume that the parameters of
Algorithm
2
satisfy
q^{*}\leqslant 1
with
\eta
and
K
held constant. Assume that
\sigma_{k}
has been chosen (and so is deterministic for the purposes of this lemma) and assume that

\max_{j}|\sigma_{k,j}|\leqslant k\eta^{1/2}n^{\alpha}.

Then, conditioned on
A,Z_{0},\dots,Z_{k-1}
, with probability at least

1-C_{1}\eta^{-1}n\exp(-C_{2}n^{2\alpha-4\delta})-C_{3}\exp\left(-C_{4}\beta^{2}n^{1-4\delta-4\alpha}+C_{5}\beta^{-1}\eta^{-1}n^{3\alpha}\right).

in the Gaussian vector
Z_{k}
, we have

\max_{j}|\sigma_{k+1,j}|\leqslant(k+1)\eta^{1/2}n^{\alpha}

and

d_{W,2}\left(\emp(\sigma_{k+1}),\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}\left[\emp(\sigma_{k+1})|A,\sigma_{1},\dots,\sigma_{k}\right]\right)\leqslant C_{7}n^{-\alpha/2}.

Remark 5.10
.

For concreteness, one may take
\alpha=1/9
and then
n^{2\alpha-4\delta}=n^{2/9-4\delta}=n^{1-4\delta-7\alpha}
.

Proof.

By
Lemma
5.7
,
\max_{j}|\sigma_{k+1,j}-\sigma_{k,j}|\leqslant\eta^{1/2}n^{\alpha}
with probability at least
1-M_{1}n\exp(-M_{2}n^{2\alpha-4\delta})
in the Gaussian vector
Z_{k}
conditioned on
A,Z_{0},\dots,Z_{k-1}
. This in particular, together with
K\eta\leqslant q^{*}\leqslant 1
, implies that

(5.10)

\max_{j}|\sigma_{k+1,j}|\leqslant(k+1)\eta^{1/2}n^{\alpha}\leqslant K\eta^{1/2}n^{\alpha}\leqslant\eta^{-1/2}n^{\alpha}

with probability at least
1-M_{1}Kn\exp(-M_{2}n^{2\alpha-4\delta})
.
Fix
\ell
such that
2^{\ell-1}\leqslant 2\eta^{-1/2}n^{\alpha}\leqslant 2^{\ell}
. We truncate the expected empirical distribution as follows: Let
\operatorname{proj}_{[-2^{\ell},2^{\ell}]}
be the truncation map

\operatorname{proj}_{[-2^{\ell},2^{\ell}]}(s)=\begin{cases}-2^{\ell},&s\in(-\infty,-2^{\ell}]\\
s,&s\in[-2^{\ell},2^{\ell}]\\
2^{\ell},&s\in[2^{\ell},\infty).\end{cases}

and let
\tau_{0}
be the pushforward of
\tau=\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}\left[\emp(\sigma_{k+1})|A,\sigma_{1},\dots,\sigma_{k}\right]
under
\operatorname{proj}_{[-2^{\ell},2^{\ell}]}
. Since
|\sigma_{k,j}|\leqslant k\eta^{1/2}n^{\alpha}\leqslant\eta^{-1/2}n^{\alpha}
by assumption, we have

|\sigma_{k+1,j}-\sigma_{k,j}|\leqslant\eta^{-1/2}n^{\alpha}\implies|\sigma_{k+1,j}|\leqslant 2\eta^{-1/2}n^{\alpha}\leqslant 2^{\ell}.

and thus

|\sigma_{k+1,j}-\proj_{[-2^{\ell},2^{\ell}]}(\sigma_{k+1,j})|\leqslant\mathbbm{1}_{|\sigma_{k+1,j}-\sigma_{k,j}|\geqslant\eta^{-1/2}n^{\alpha}}|\sigma_{k+1,j}-\sigma_{k,j}|.

Note that
\sigma_{k+1,j}
is Gaussian with mean
\sigma_{k,j}
and variance bounded by
M\eta n^{4\delta}
by
Proposition
5.3

Item
5
. Therefore, by Lemma
5.8
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\mathbbm{1}_{|\sigma_{k+1,j}-\sigma_{k,j}|\geqslant\eta^{-1/2}n^{\alpha}}(\sigma_{k+1,j}-\sigma_{k,j})^{2}]

\displaystyle\leqslant\frac{2M\eta n^{4\delta}}{\sqrt{2\pi}}(M^{-1/2}\eta^{-1}n^{\alpha-\delta}+M^{1/2}\eta n^{\delta-\alpha})e^{-M^{-1}\eta^{-1}n^{-2\delta}\eta^{-1}n^{2\alpha}}

\displaystyle\leqslant 2M^{3/2}n^{\alpha+3\delta}e^{-M^{-1}\eta^{-2}n^{2\alpha-2\delta}}.

Therefore,

\displaystyle d_{W,2}(\tau,\tau_{0})^{2}

\displaystyle\leqslant\int_{|x|\geqslant\eta^{-1/2}n^{\alpha}}\left(x-\operatorname{proj}_{[-2^{\ell},2^{\ell}]}(x)\right)^{2}\,d\tau(x)

\displaystyle\leqslant\frac{1}{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\mathbb{E}\left[|\sigma_{k+1,j}-\proj_{[-2^{\ell},2^{\ell}]}(\sigma_{k+1,j})|^{2}\right]

\displaystyle\leqslant\frac{1}{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbbm{1}_{|\sigma_{k+1,j}-\sigma_{k,j}|\geqslant\eta^{-1/2}n^{\alpha}}(\sigma_{k+1,j}-\sigma_{k,j})^{2}\right]

\displaystyle\leqslant 2M^{3/2}n^{\alpha+3\delta}e^{-M^{-1}\eta^{-2}n^{2\alpha-2\delta}}

(5.11)

\displaystyle\leqslant M^{\prime}n^{-\alpha},

where we choose
M^{\prime}
such that
M^{\prime}\geqslant 2M^{3/2}n^{2\alpha+3\delta}e^{-M^{-1}\eta^{-2}n^{2\alpha-2\delta}}
by choosing
M^{\prime}=2M^{3/2}n_{0}e^{-M^{-1}n_{0}^{1/9}}
for
n_{0}
solving
0=\frac{d}{dn_{0}}\left(n_{0}e^{-M^{-1}n_{0}^{1/9}}\right)
.

Now we proceed to estimate
d_{W,1}(\emp(\sigma_{k+1}),\tau_{0})
. Take
f\in\mathsf{Lip}_{1}([-2^{\ell},2^{\ell}])
, and extend
f
to an
1
-Lipschitz function on
\mathbb{R}
by setting
f
to be constant on
(-\infty,-2^{\ell}]
and
[2^{\ell},\infty)
, so that
f\circ\proj_{[-2^{\ell},2^{\ell}]}=f
. Consider

F_{f}(Z_{k})=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}f\left(\sigma_{k,j}+\eta^{1/2}(Q_{k}Z_{k})_{j}\right)

where
Z_{k}
is a standard Gaussian vector
and recall from the proof of
Lemma
5.5
that the distribution of
\sigma_{k,j}+\eta^{1/2}(Q_{k}Z_{k})
over the randomness of
j
and
Z_{k}
is equal to
\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}\left[\emp(\sigma_{k+1})|A,\sigma_{1},\dots,\sigma_{k}\right]
, so
\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}_{Z_{k}}[F_{f}(Z_{k})]=\int f\,d\tau=\int(f\circ\proj_{[-2^{\ell},2^{\ell}]})d\tau=\int f\,d\tau_{0}
. Moreover,
F_{f}(Z_{k})
is an
\eta^{1/2}\left\lVert{Q_{k}}\right\rVert/\sqrt{n}
-Lipschitz function of
Z_{k}
, since

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\left|f\left(\sigma_{k,j}+\eta^{1/2}(Q_{k}Z_{k})_{j}\right)-f\left(\sigma_{k,j}+\eta^{1/2}(Q_{k}Z_{k}^{\prime})_{j}\right)\right|

\displaystyle\leqslant\frac{1}{n}\eta^{1/2}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\left|(Q_{k}(Z_{k}-Z_{k}^{\prime}))_{j}\right|

\displaystyle=\frac{\eta^{1/2}}{n}|Q_{k}(Z_{k}-Z_{k}^{\prime})|_{1}\leqslant\frac{\eta^{1/2}}{\sqrt{n}}\left\lVert{Q_{k}}\right\rVert\,|Z_{k}-Z_{k}^{\prime}|_{2}\,.

Now we apply the concentration estimate to the Lipschitz function
F_{f}(Z_{k})
above, while noting that
\left\lVert{Q_{k}}\right\rVert n^{-1/2}\leqslant M_{5}n^{2\delta-1/2}
by
Proposition
5.3

Item
5
. By concentration of Lipschitz functions of Gaussians (i.e.
Lemma
4.14
with
n=1
), we have

\left|F_{f}(Z_{k})-\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}_{Z_{k}}\left[F_{f}(Z_{k})\right]\right|\leqslant\frac{1}{2^{m}}

with probability at least

1-M_{6}\exp\left(-M_{7}n^{1-4\delta}2^{-2m}\eta^{-1}\right).

Choose
m
so that
2^{-m}\leqslant\beta\eta^{1/2}n^{-2\alpha}\leqslant 2^{-m+1}
. By
Lemma
5.6
and
Corollary
B.3
, we can estimate the
L^{1}
Wasserstein distance between measures supported in
[-2^{\ell},2^{\ell}]
up to an error of size
1/2^{m}
by testing only the
1
-Lipschitz functions
f
in some set
\mathcal{F}
satisfying
|\mathcal{F}|=2^{2^{\ell+m+1}}
. We use a union bound for the probability of error for each of these Lipschitz functions
f
.
This yields

\displaystyle d_{W,1}(\emp(\sigma_{k+1}),\tau_{0})

\displaystyle=\sup_{\left\lVert{f}\right\rVert_{\mathrm{Lip}}\leqslant 1}\left|\int f(d\emp(\sigma_{k+1})-d\tau_{0})\right|

\displaystyle\leqslant\frac{1}{2^{m}}+\sup_{f\in\mathcal{F}}\left|\int f(d\emp(\sigma_{k+1})-d\tau_{0})\right|

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2^{m}}+\sup_{f\in\mathcal{F}}\left|F_{f}(Z_{k})-\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}_{Z_{k}}\left[F_{f}(Z_{k})\right]\right|

\displaystyle\leqslant\frac{2}{2^{m}}\leqslant 2\eta^{1/2}n^{-2\alpha}

with probability at least

(5.12)

1-M_{6}2^{2^{\ell+m+1}}\exp\left(-M_{7}n^{1-4\delta}2^{-2m}\eta^{-1}\right),

provided that
\max_{j}|\sigma_{k+1,j}|\leqslant\eta^{-1/2}n^{\alpha}\leqslant 2^{\ell}
which by
(
5.10
)
happens with probability at least

(5.13)

1-M_{1}Kn\exp(-M_{2}n^{2\alpha-4\delta}).

Hence, in this high-probability event, by Hölder’s inequality,

d_{W,2}(\emp(\sigma_{k+1}),\tau_{0})\leqslant 2^{\ell/2}d_{W,1}(\emp(\sigma_{k+1}),\tau_{0})^{1/2}\leqslant M_{8}\eta^{-1/4}n^{\alpha/2}\eta^{1/4}n^{-\alpha}=M_{8}n^{-\alpha/2},

which combines with
(
5.11
)
by triangle inequality to obtain

(5.14)

d_{W,2}(\emp(\sigma_{k+1}),\tau)\leqslant M_{9}n^{-\alpha/2}.

It remains to evaluate the probability
(
5.12
)
coming from concentration. By construction,
2^{\ell}\leqslant 4\eta^{-1/2}n^{\alpha}
and
2^{m}\leqslant 2\beta^{-1}\eta^{-1/2}n^{2\alpha}
. Thus,

2^{2^{\ell+m+1}}\leqslant M_{9}\exp\left(M_{10}\beta^{-1}\eta^{-1}n^{3\alpha}\right).

Hence,

\displaystyle M_{6}2^{2^{\ell+m+1}}

\displaystyle\exp\left(-M_{7}n^{1-4\delta}2^{-2m}\eta^{-1}\right)

\displaystyle\leqslant M_{6}M_{9}\exp\bigg(M_{10}\beta^{-1}\eta^{-1}n^{3\alpha}\bigg)\exp\left(-M_{7}n^{1-4\delta}2^{-2m}\eta^{-1}\right)

\displaystyle\leqslant M_{11}\exp\left(-M_{12}\beta^{2}n^{1-4\delta-4\alpha}+M_{10}\beta^{-1}\eta^{-1}n^{3\alpha}\right),

which, when combined with
(
5.12
)
and
(
5.13
)
, yields the final probability of the bounds
(
5.10
)
and
(
5.14
)
.
∎

5.4.
Conclusion of the convergence argument

We are ready to finish proving
Theorem
5.1
. As preparation, we record a small computation for the inductive step.

Corollary 5.11
.

Let
\delta\in(0,1/19]
, and let
\alpha\in(2\delta,1/7-4\delta/7)
. Assume that the parameters
\eta
and
n
satisfy
n^{1/90}\eta\geqslant 1
. Let
A
satisfy the conclusions of
Theorem
4.1
.
Assume that
\max_{j}|\sigma_{k,j}|\leqslant k\eta^{1/2}n^{\alpha}
, that
2d_{W,2}(\emp(\sigma_{k}),\operatorname{dist}(Y_{t_{k}}^{\gamma}))+(1+e^{5\beta^{2}t_{k}})\gamma\leqslant\frac{1}{2\sqrt{2}\beta}
, and moreover that

d_{W,2}(\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}\left[\emp(\sigma_{k+1})|A,\sigma_{1},\dots,\sigma_{k}\right],\operatorname{dist}(Y_{t_{k+1}}^{\gamma}))\leqslant 1.

Then, conditioned on
A,Z_{0},\dots,Z_{k-1}
, with probability in the Gaussian vector
Z_{k}
of at least

1-C_{1}n\eta^{-1}\exp\left(-C_{2}n^{2/9-4\delta}\right),

we have both

d_{W,2}\left(\emp(\sigma_{k+1}),\operatorname{dist}(Y^{\gamma}_{t_{k+1}})\right)^{2}-d_{W,2}\left(\emp(\sigma_{k}),\operatorname{dist}(Y^{\gamma}_{t_{k}})\right)^{2}\\
\leqslant\eta\cdot\left[C_{4}\beta^{2}d_{W,2}\left(\emp(\sigma_{k}),\operatorname{dist}(Y^{\gamma}_{t_{k}})\right)^{2}+C_{5}\beta^{6}\eta+C_{6}\beta^{2}n^{-\delta}+C_{7}\beta^{2}e^{10\beta^{2}}\gamma^{2}+C_{8}\eta^{-1}n^{-1/18}\right].

and

\max_{j}|\sigma_{k+1,j}|\leqslant(k+1)\eta^{1/2}n^{\alpha}.

Proof.

By triangle inequality and making the conditioning in
\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}\left[\emp(\sigma_{k+1})|A,\sigma_{1},\dots,\sigma_{k}\right]
notationally implicit for brevity,

\displaystyle d_{W,2}\left(\emp(\sigma_{k+1}),\operatorname{dist}(Y^{\gamma}_{t_{k+1}})\right)^{2}

\displaystyle\leqslant\left(d_{W,2}\left(\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}\emp(\sigma_{k+1}),\operatorname{dist}(Y^{\gamma}_{t_{k+1}})\right)+d_{W,2}\left(\emp(\sigma_{k+1}),\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}\emp(\sigma_{k+1})\right)\right)^{2}

\displaystyle=d_{W,2}\left(\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}\emp(\sigma_{k+1}),\operatorname{dist}(Y^{\gamma}_{t_{k+1}})\right)^{2}

\displaystyle\quad+2d_{W,2}\left(\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}\emp(\sigma_{k+1}),\operatorname{dist}(Y^{\gamma}_{t_{k+1}})\right)d_{W,2}\left(\emp(\sigma_{k+1}),\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}\emp(\sigma_{k+1})\right)

\displaystyle\quad+d_{W,2}\left(\emp(\sigma_{k+1}),\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}\emp(\sigma_{k+1})\right)^{2}.

Assuming that
d_{W,2}(\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}\emp(\sigma_{k+1}),\operatorname{dist}(Y^{\gamma}_{t_{k+1}}))\leqslant 1
, we can bound

2d_{W,2}\left(\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}\emp(\sigma_{k+1}),\operatorname{dist}(Y^{\gamma}_{t_{k+1}})\right)d_{W,2}\left(\emp(\sigma_{k+1}),\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}\emp(\sigma_{k+1})\right)+d_{W,2}\left(\emp(\sigma_{k+1}),\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}\emp(\sigma_{k+1})\right)^{2}\\
\leqslant M_{1}n^{-\alpha/2}+M_{2}n^{-\alpha},

using
Lemma
5.9
with
\alpha=1/9
. Combining this with
Lemma
5.5
using triangle inequality, we obtain the asserted estimate with probability

1-M_{3}\eta^{-1}n\exp\left(-M_{4}n^{2/9-4\delta}\right)-M_{5}\exp\left(-M_{6}n^{5/9-4\delta}+M_{7}\beta^{-1}\eta^{-1}n^{3/9}\right).

By our assumptions
\beta\geqslant 1
and
\eta n^{1/90}\geqslant 1
, and hence
\beta^{-1}\eta^{-1}n^{3/9}\leqslant n^{31/90}
. Since
-M_{6}n^{5/9-4\delta}+M_{7}n^{31/90}-M_{8}\leqslant-M_{9}n^{2/9-4\delta}
, we can absorb the
M_{5}\exp\left(-M_{6}n^{5/9-4\delta}+M_{7}n^{31/90}\right)
term into the
M_{3}\eta^{-1}n\exp(-M_{4}n^{2/9-4\delta})
term.
∎

Now we are ready to finish the argument.

Proof of
Theorem
5.1
.

We induct over
k=0,\dots,k^{\prime}-1
.
Write
\zeta_{n,\beta,\eta,\gamma}:=M_{2}\beta^{4}\eta+M_{3}n^{-\delta}+M_{4}e^{10\beta^{2}}\gamma^{2}+M_{5}\beta^{-2}\eta^{-1}n^{-1/18}
and
\Delta_{k}:=d_{W,2}(\emp(\sigma_{k}),\operatorname{dist}(Y_{t_{k}}^{\gamma}))
.
The inductive hypothesis will be that with probability at least
1-M_{6}n(k-1)\eta^{-1}\exp\left(-M_{7}n^{2/9-4\delta}\right)
,

(5.15)

\Delta_{k}^{2}\leqslant\sum_{j=0}^{k-1}(1+M_{1}\eta\beta^{2})^{j}\eta\beta^{2}\cdot\zeta_{n,\beta,\eta,\gamma}

and that the hypotheses of
Corollary
5.11
will hold for this value of
k
with
\alpha=1/9
.

Note that
\Delta_{0}=0
since
\sigma_{0}=0
and
Y_{0}=0
